Potentials of axisymmetric razor-thin disks
J. An

Abstract
We investigate the gravitational potentials generated by axisymmetric, razor-thin disks. Within certain limitations, the potential on one side of the disk is shown to be equivalent to the potential produced by a linear mass distribution along the axis perpendicular to the disk. We first establish the connection between two mass distributions that generate the same potential. We then consider all disk surface density profiles that produce the potential equivalent to those generated by linear mass distributions, specifically those defined by the elementary beta distribution and its relatives on the interval [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] , [ 1 , ∞ ) [1,\infty) or [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) . These families of models are important because the potentials in all cases are given by, at most, a single real quadrature of elementary functions of the coordinates, and furthermore, many cases result in closed-form expressions. The potential of many realistic disks may be constructed from some combinations of these disk models.



I
Introduction

Despite their elementary nature, calculating the gravitational potentials of completely flattened mass distributions has proven to be a significant challenge. Although integrating the Newtonian potential of mass elements is, in principle, straightforward, one immediately discovers that such calculations typically involve multiple integrals or integral transformations with the elliptic integral kernels (which effectively add another layer of integration) even for simple, circularly symmetric surface density profiles. If one were to construct a family of orbits around such a disk, the computational demand quickly becomes impractical. Consequently, many settle for a compromise, modeling the disk with profiles that generate simpler potentials. Still, a striking dearth of known exact expressions for the potentials generated by disks persists, even when the surface mass density is circularly symmetric.

This paper provides a new physical interpretation of the method for finding the potential of a circularly symmetric, razor-thin disk, originally attributed to
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
. Subsequently, this method is extended to its full generality by means of the Mellin transform. We then construct a large array of circular disk models with exact analytical expressions for their potentials, which may be considered as a generalization of the model set first proposed by
Qian 1992
. Ultimately, the current paper serves as the opening chapter of a compendium of the “analytic” disk potentials, providing both the requisite mathematical machinery and a resource for modeling various highly flattened astronomical objects.

II
Preliminary

II.1
The Mestel disk

Let us consider the potential generated by a semi-infinite uniform string of mass. If it coincides with the negative-
z
part of the
R=0
axis, the gravitational potential
\Phi
it generates in cylindrical polar coordinates
(R,\phi,z)
can be written down as

\Phi(R,z)=-G\Lambda\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}h}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(z+h)^{2}}},

(II.1)

where
\Lambda
is the linear mass density of the string. Although one finds that equation (
II.1
) does not converge, the resulting gravitational acceleration
\bm{\varg}=-\bm{\nabla}\Phi
is nevertheless well-defined:

\frac{-\bm{\varg}}{G\Lambda}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{R\,\hat{\bm{e}}_{R}+(z+h)\,{\hat{\Bbbk\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu}{}}{\left[R^{2}+(z+h)^{2}\right]^{3/2}}\mathrm{d}h=\left(1-\frac{z}{r}\right)\frac{\hat{\bm{e}}_{R}}{R}+\frac{{\hat{\Bbbk\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu}{}}{r},

(II.2)

where
\hat{\bm{e}}_{R}=\bm{\nabla}R
and
{\hat{\Bbbk\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu}{}=\bm{\nabla}z
are the unit vectors in the increasing
R
and
z
directions, and
r^{2}=R^{2}+z^{2}
. In principle, the explicit expression for
\Phi(R,z)
may be obtained by integrating equation (
II.2
) back. Instead let us consider the equipotential surfaces first. If
\hat{\bm{e}}_{r}=\bm{\nabla}r
is the radial unit vector, we find
r\hat{\bm{e}}_{r}=R\hat{\bm{e}}_{R}+z{\hat{\Bbbk\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu}{}
and so

\frac{\bm{\nabla}\Phi}{G\Lambda}=\frac{r-z}{R^{2}}(\hat{\bm{e}}_{r}+{\hat{\Bbbk\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu}{})=\frac{\bm{\nabla}(r+z)}{r+z},

(II.3)

that is, the equipotential surfaces are also the set of surfaces with constant
r+z
. The meridional cross-sections of these surfaces are convex-upward confocal parabolae with the focus at the origin and the
R=0
axis being its symmetry axis (see
An 2013
, sect. 5.1). Hence the potential due to a semi-infinite uniform string of mass is stratified over the family of the confocal paraboloids of revolution. The potential itself is a function of
s=r+z
alone,
\Phi(R,z)=f(s)
, and so
\bm{\nabla}\Phi=f^{\prime}(s)(\bm{\nabla}s)=f^{\prime}(s)(\hat{\bm{e}}_{r}+{\hat{\Bbbk\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu}{})
. Then from equation (
II.3
),
f^{\prime}(s)=G\Lambda/s
and
f(s)=G\Lambda\ln s+\text{constant}
. Consequently

\Phi(R,z)=G\Lambda\ln\left(\frac{z+\!\sqrt{R^{2}+z^{2}}}{2a}\right)

(II.4)

up to an arbitrary zero-point
a
. Formally if the unintegrable singularity was removed in equation (
II.1
) by introducing an infinite offset, one obtains the same result:

\frac{\Phi(R,z)}{G\Lambda}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}h\,\left(\frac{1}{a+h}-\frac{1}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(z+h)^{2}}}\right).

(II.5)

Equation (
II.4
) satisfies the Laplace equation (
\nabla^{2}\Phi=0
) everywhere except the negative-
z
part of the
R=0
axis, where it is singular. The same function therefore corresponds to the potential due to some localized mass distributions in the source-free part of the space, subject to the boundary conditions. In particular, an axisymmetric harmonic potential
\Phi(R,z)
in the upper half-space (
z>0
) and the reflection-symmetric function in the lower half-space results from an infinitesimally thin disk on the mid-plane (
z=0
) whose surface mass density
\Sigma(R)
is given by
2\pi G\Sigma(R)=\lim_{z\downarrow 0^{+}}{\hat{\Bbbk\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu}{}\bm{\cdot}\bm{\nabla}\Phi
. Since
{\hat{\Bbbk\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu}{}\bm{\cdot}\bm{\nabla}\Phi=\partial\Phi/\partial z=G\Lambda/r
for
\Phi(R,z)
in equation (
II.4
), the razor-thin disk with the surface density given by

\Sigma(R)=\frac{\varv_{0}^{2}}{2\pi GR}

(II.6)

therefore generates the potential behaving like

\Phi(R,z)=\varv_{0}^{2}\ln\left(\frac{|z|+\!\sqrt{R^{2}+z^{2}}}{2a}\right)

(II.7)

where
a
is again an arbitrary integration constant. Note that the circular speed is given by
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)=\lim_{z\downarrow 0^{+}}R(\partial\Phi/\partial R)=G\Lambda=\varv_{0}^{2}
, and so this model is equivalent to the scale-free Mestel disk
Mestel 1963
, which is characterized by a flat mid-plane rotation curve. In other words, the equipotential surfaces of the scale-free Mestel disk are stratified over the set of confocal paraboloids of revolution whose foci are at the center of the disk. This fact contrasts with the Kepler potential due to a point mass, which is stratified over the set of concentric spherical surfaces, as well as the plane-parallel potential due to an infinitely-extended uniform plate (see
Routh 1892
, sect. 22). Furthermore, the potential and the gravity field of the scale-free Mestel disk on one side of the disk are indistinguishable from those due to the uniform string of mass extending from the center infinitely to the opposite side in the perpendicular direction to the disk. This last fact is again compared with the Kuzmin disk
Kuzmin 1953
;
Kuzmin 1956
whose potential on one side of the disk reproduces the potential of a point mass located at a position on the symmetry axis on the opposite side.

A similar procedure for obtaining the Kuzmin disk potential from the Kepler potential may be applied to the scale-free Mestel disk potential. That is to say, consider displacing the potential of the scale-free Mestel disk farther along the symmetry axis and reflecting it off with respect to the mid-plane. This is equivalent to reflecting equation (
II.4
) with respect to the plane
z=c>0
, which is now interpreted as the new plane of the disk. The surface density and the mid-plane rotation curve of the corresponding disk are then

\Sigma(R)=\frac{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi G\!\sqrt{R^{2}+c^{2}}};\hskip 10.00002pt\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)=\varv_{\infty}^{2}\left(1-\frac{c}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+c^{2}}}\right),

(II.8)

where
\varv_{\infty}^{2}
is the scale constant setting the asymptotic rotation speed due to the disk. This is the Mestel disk modified by replacing its
R^{-1}
central cusp with a flat-topped core,
\Sigma(0)=(2\pi Gc)^{-1}\varv_{\infty}^{2}
, of a “core radius”
c
. This “cored Mestel disk” is also recognized as the
n=0
member of the
Toomre 1963
’s disk family
(
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
)
. The explicit expression of the potential due to the cored Mestel disk is given by equation (
II.7
) with
|z|\to|z|+c
,

\Phi(R,z)=\varv_{\infty}^{2}\ln\left(\frac{|z|+c+\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+c)^{2}}}{2a}\right).

(II.9)

Obviously the
c=0
limit recovers the potential of the scale-free Mestel disk
(
Lynden-Bell 1989
)
in equation (
II.7
). While the potential diverges as
r\to\infty
, the central value
\Phi(0,0)
is finite, which may be set to zero by choosing
a=c
. The equipotential surfaces of the cored Mestel disk in the upper half-space are stratified over the set of confocal paraboloids of revolution but the common foci of the paraboloids are displaced along the symmetry axis to the point
z=-c
on the opposite side of the disk.

II.2
The Mestel-difference disk

The paraboloid of revolution is the limiting case of a prolate spheroid as one of its foci is sent to the point at infinity. By contrast, the sphere is a prolate spheroid whose two foci coincide. In other words, the equipotential surfaces of the Kuzmin and Mestel disks are composed of the special class of prolate spheroids. Generalizing these, we would like to find the disks that generate the potential stratified over more general classes of the confocal prolate spheroids.

If the symmetry axis of the prolate spheroid is on the
R=0
axis, the meridional cross-section of the spheroid becomes an ellipse whose long axis is also on the
R=0
axis. Since an ellipse is a locus such that the sum of the distances to the two foci is constant (and the line joining the two foci is on its major axis), a member of the family of the confocal prolate spheroids whose foci are located
z=-c_{1}
and
z=-c_{2}
on the
R=0
axis with
c_{1}>c_{2}\geq 0
is identified with the sum of the distances to each focus
r_{1}+r_{2}
being constant, where
r_{1}^{2}=R^{2}+(z+c_{1})^{2}
and
r_{2}^{2}=R^{2}+(z+c_{2})^{2}
. Suppose that these spheroids constitute the equipotential surfaces for
z>0
due to an infinitesimally thin disk placed in the
z=0
plane. The potential for
z>0
is a function of
s=r_{1}+r_{2}
that is also a solution of the Poisson equation. However, since the space with
z\neq 0
is empty,
\Phi=f(r_{1}+r_{2})
should actually satisfy

\nabla^{2}\Phi=\frac{1}{r_{1}r_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\left\{\bigl[s^{2}-(c_{1}-c_{2})^{2}\bigr]\frac{\mathrm{d}f}{\mathrm{d}s}\right\}=0,

which reduces to an ordinary differential equation on
f(s)
, the general solution of which is found to be (
s>c_{1}-c_{2}>0
)

f(s)=-C\tanh^{-1}\!\left(\frac{c_{1}-c_{2}}{s}\right)

with an integration constant
C
. Since
f(s)\simeq-(c_{1}-c_{2})C/s
as
s\to\infty
, the choice of the potential (with
c_{1}>c_{2}\geq 0
)

\Phi=-\frac{2GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{c_{1}-c_{2}}\tanh^{-1}\!\left(\frac{c_{1}-c_{2}}{r_{1}+r_{2}}\right)

(II.10)

where
r_{j}^{2}=R^{2}+(|z|+c_{j})^{2}
, exhibits the behavior,
\Phi\sim-GM_{\mathrm{t}}/r
as
r\to\infty
consistent with a localized mass distribution. Equation (
II.10
) is found to be generated by the razor-thin disk in the
z=0
plane with

2\pi G\Sigma(R)=\lim_{z\downarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z}=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{c_{1}-c_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{R_{2}}-\frac{1}{R_{1}}\right)

(II.11)

where
R_{j}^{2}=R^{2}+c_{j}^{2}
. Provided
c_{1}>c_{2}\geq 0
, we find
\Sigma(R)>0
for all
R\geq 0
and
4\pi\Sigma\simeq(c_{1}+c_{2})M_{\mathrm{t}}R^{-3}
as
R\to\infty
, implying a finite total mass,
M_{\mathrm{t}}=2\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}R\,R\Sigma(R)
. At the center, the surface density and the potential well depth are both finite with
\Sigma(0)=M_{\mathrm{t}}/(2\pi c_{1}c_{2})
and
|\Phi(0,0)|=GM_{\mathrm{t}}\ln(c_{1}/c_{2})/(c_{1}-c_{2})
unless
c_{2}=0
. If
c_{1}>c_{2}=0
however,

\Sigma(R)=\frac{M_{\mathrm{t}}}{2\pi c_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{R}-\frac{1}{R_{1}}\right),

exhibits an
R^{-1}
singularity and the potential diverges logarithmically as
R\to 0
, like those of the scale-free Mestel disk.

In fact, equation (
II.11
) is simply the difference between two Mestel disks with the same asymptotic circular speed but different core radii (with
c_{2}=0
for the scale-free Mestel disk), while the
c_{1}\to\infty
limit is the Mestel disk (of infinite mass and indeterminate
M_{\mathrm{t}}/c_{1}
) with the core radius of
c_{2}\geq 0
. The circular speed for this “Mestel-difference disk” is found to be

\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{c_{1}-c_{2}}\left(\frac{c_{1}}{R_{1}}-\frac{c_{2}}{R_{2}}\right),

which is the difference of
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
in equation (
II.8
). Given the finite total mass (
c_{1}\neq 0
), this rotation curve falls off like
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)\simeq GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R
as
R\to\infty
, while the maximum occurs at
R
where
R^{2}=c_{1}^{4/3}c_{2}^{2/3}+c_{1}^{2/3}c_{2}^{4/3}=c_{1}^{2/3}c_{2}^{2/3}(c_{1}^{2/3}+c_{2}^{2/3})
with

\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c},\max}^{2}}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}=\frac{c_{1}+c_{2}}{\bigl[c_{1}^{4/3}+(c_{1}c_{2})^{2/3}+c_{2}^{4/3}\bigr]^{3/2}}.

The rotation curve for
c_{1}>c_{2}>0
behaves like
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)\sim R^{2}
as
R\to 0
, but the circular speed for
c_{1}>c_{2}=0
monotonically decreases from the finite maximum at the center
\varv_{\mathrm{c},\max}^{2}=\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(0)=GM_{\mathrm{t}}/c_{1}
. The result for the
c_{1}\to\infty
limit (the Mestel disk) is found in equation (
II.8
), for which
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
increases monotonically (unless
c_{2}=0
too) and tends to a finite limiting value (asymptotically flat).

In the
c=c_{1}=c_{2}>0
limit maintaining
M_{\mathrm{t}}
constant, it is easy to observe that the Mestel-difference disk reduces to

\Sigma(R)=\frac{M_{\mathrm{t}}}{2\pi}\frac{c}{(R^{2}+c^{2})^{3/2}};\hskip 10.00002pt\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}R^{2}}{(R^{2}+c^{2})^{3/2}},

which is actually the Kuzmin disk, the potential of which follows the same
c=c_{1}=c_{2}
limit of equation (
II.10
); that is,

\Phi(R,z)=-\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+c)^{2}}}.

(II.12)

All of the limiting behaviors of the Kuzmin disk are consistent with it being a member of the Mestel-difference disks.

III
The Evans–de Zeeuw method

As the potential of the Mestel disk on one side is identical to that generated by a semi-infinite string on the opposite side, the potential of the Mestel-difference disk (which is stratified over the confocal prolate spheroids) is also indistinguishable from the difference of the potentials generated by two semi-infinite strings of the same linear mass density. That is to say, the potential of the Mestel-difference disk on one side is the same as that due to a string of uniform linear mass density of a finite length,
c_{1}-c_{2}
. In fact, suppose that the potential is given by
\Phi=-C\tanh^{-1}[(c_{1}-c_{2})/(r_{1}+r_{2})]
in the whole space, except for
z\in[-c_{1},-c_{2}]
and
R=0
, for which
r_{1}+r_{2}=c_{1}-c_{2}
and so
\Phi
becomes singular. Integrating the Poisson equation over the region where the potential is singular, we find
\Phi
is consistent with the boundary condition,
2G\Lambda(z)=\lim_{R\to 0}R\hat{\bm{e}}_{R}\bm{\cdot}\bm{\nabla}\Phi=C
for
z\in[-c_{1},-c_{2}]
(so
c_{2}+z\leq 0\leq c_{1}+z
) with
\Lambda(z)
being the constant linear mass density. The two ends of the string equivalent to the Mestel-difference disk are located at
z=-c_{1},-c_{2}
on the
R=0
axis, and so the total mass of the equivalent string is also the same as that of the corresponding Mestel-difference disk
M_{\mathrm{t}}=(c_{1}-c_{2})\Lambda
.

Generalizing this, consider the pair of a razor-thin axisymmetric disk and a linear mass distribution along the symmetry axis on the opposite side that generates the identical potential on one side of the disk. The potential is expressible as a simple integral quadrature of the linear mass density
\Lambda
:

\Phi(R,z)=-\int_{I}\frac{G\Lambda(h)\,\mathrm{d}h}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+h)^{2}}}

(III.1)

where
h\in I\subset[0,\infty)
, whereas the surface density of the corresponding disk and the resulting rotation curve are:

\begin{split}\Sigma(R)&=\frac{1}{2\pi G}\lim_{z\downarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{I}\frac{h\Lambda(h)\,\mathrm{d}h}{(R^{2}+h^{2})^{3/2}};\\
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)&=\lim_{z\downarrow 0^{+}}R\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial R}=GR^{2}\int_{I}\frac{\Lambda(h)\,\mathrm{d}h}{(R^{2}+h^{2})^{3/2}}.\end{split}

(III.2)

This approach is basically equivalent to the method proposed by
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
, who considered decomposing the surface mass density of the disk to a weighted integral over the set of the Kuzmin disks with varying scale lengths. What has been shown is that we may assign a physical interpretation on the weight function of
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
(henceforth
1
) as the linear mass density of the string that can generate the same potential as the circularly symmetric razor-thin disk under consideration.

III.1
How to get
\Lambda
from
\Sigma

The main difficulty involved with this technique is that finding the weight
\Lambda
from the known surface density is non-trivial, whereas obtaining the potential and surface density from a given
\Lambda
is straightforward. Here we consider a few inversion methods, although none is entirely satisfactory.

First we extend the support of the integral in equation (
III.2
) to
I\to[0,\infty)
by assuming
\Lambda(h)=0
for
h\notin I
. This allows us to consider the integral transformation over the standard interval
[0,\infty)
. Also note that the transformation for
\Lambda\to\Sigma
is formally equivalent to that for
h^{-1}\Lambda\to R^{-2}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
. Hence the provided inversion method for
\Sigma\to\Lambda
is equally applicable to
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\to\Lambda
after obvious adjustments.

III.1.1
The Laplace transform

Let us observe that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{e}^{-x(R^{2}+h^{2})}\!\sqrt{x}=\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}}{2(R^{2}+h^{2})^{3/2}}.

Thus equation (
III.2
) may be reducible to

\begin{split}\Sigma(R)&=\frac{1}{\pi^{3/2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}h\,h\Lambda(h)\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{e}^{-x(R^{2}+h^{2})}\!\sqrt{x}\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi^{3/2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{e}^{-xR^{2}}\!\sqrt{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}(h^{2})\,\mathrm{e}^{-xh^{2}}\Lambda(h).\end{split}

This is recognized as an iterated Laplace transform;

2\pi^{\frac{3}{2}}\Sigma(R)=\underset{x\to R^{2}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left\{\!\sqrt{x}\underset{h^{2}\to x}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left[\Lambda(h)\right]\right\},

and so we can at least formally invert this for
\Lambda
:

\Lambda(h)=2\pi^{\frac{3}{2}}\underset{x\to h^{2}}{\mathcal{L}^{-1}}\!\left\{x^{-\frac{1}{2}}\underset{R^{2}\to x}{\mathcal{L}^{-1}}\!\left[\Sigma(R)\right]\right\}.

Without an explicit algorithm for the inverse Laplace transform, this formula is purely pedagogical. Although the inverse transformations are expressible explicitly via the Bromwich integrals, the resulting formula can be reduced to the direct inversion formula to be presented later, which is better understood through alternative approaches.

III.1.2
The Abel–Stieltjes transform

The right-hand sides of equations (
III.2
) are recognized as the generalized Stieltjes transform (GST)
h^{2}\to R^{2}

(
Erdélyi et al. 1954
, chap. XIV)
. Although the inversion formulae for the GST are known
(
Widder 1938
;
Schwarz 2005
)
(see also Appendix
A
), it is instructive to approach this through the canonical Stieltjes transform first:

\mathscr{S}[g(t);x]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{g(t)\,\mathrm{d}t}{t+x}=f(x).

(III.3)

which is invertible through the Stieltjes–Perron formula:

\begin{split}g(t)&=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0^{+}}\frac{f(-t-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)-f(-t+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\quad\text{for $t\in(0,\infty)$}\\
&=\frac{f(t\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\pi})-f(t\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi})}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}=\frac{1}{\pi}\bigl\lvert\Im[f(-t)]\bigr\rvert\end{split}

(III.4)

assuming that the analytic continuation of
f(x)
has a branch cut along the negative real line. The last formula with
\Im
being the imaginary part is valid assuming
f(\bar{x})=\overline{f(x)}
, which follows
g(t)\in\mathbb{R}
for
t\in(0,\infty)
.

Next we observe that the GST of any order is reducible to the canonical Stieltjes transform utilizing fractional calculus. In particular, consider the Abel transform – which is equivalent to the Weyl integral of order one-half – of equation (
III.2
):

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}^{-}(r)&=\int_{r}^{\infty}\!\frac{R\Sigma(R)\,\mathrm{d}R}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}-r^{2}}}\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}h\,h\Lambda(h)\int_{r}^{\infty}\!\frac{R\,\mathrm{d}R}{\!\sqrt{(R^{2}-r^{2})(R^{2}+h^{2})^{3}}}\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{h\Lambda(h)\,\mathrm{d}h}{r^{2}+h^{2}}=\frac{\mathscr{S}[\Lambda(\!\sqrt{h^{2}});r^{2}]}{4\pi},\end{split}

which is in the form of the Stieltjes transform (
h^{2}\to r^{2}
). So

\begin{split}\Lambda(h)&=\frac{2}{\mathrm{i}}\left[\mathcal{A}^{-}(h\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2}})-\mathcal{A}^{-}(h\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2}})\right]\\
&=\frac{2}{\mathrm{i}}\!\int\limits_{h\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\pi/2}}^{h\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi/2}}\!\frac{R\Sigma(R)\,\mathrm{d}R}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+h^{2}}},\end{split}

(III.5)

where the last integral is along the complex contour starting and ending at the points
R=h\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi/2}=\pm\mathrm{i}h
on the imaginary axis. Unless
\Sigma(R)
falls off faster than
\sim R^{-1}
as
R\to\infty
, the Abel transform
\mathcal{A}^{-}
does not converge, but we may consider another Abel transform (equivalent to the Riemann–Liouville integral of order one-half):

\mathcal{A}^{+}(r)=\int_{0}^{r}\!\frac{R\Sigma(R)\,\mathrm{d}R}{\!\sqrt{r^{2}-R^{2}}}=\frac{r}{2\pi}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\Lambda(h)\,\mathrm{d}h}{r^{2}+h^{2}}.

The resulting transformation
h^{-1}\Lambda(h)\to 4\pi r^{-1}\mathcal{A}^{+}(r)
is also recognized as the Stieltjes transform (
h^{2}\to r^{2}
), and equation (
III.4
) thus leads to

\Lambda(h)=2\mathrm{i}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2}}\mathcal{A}^{+}(h\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2}})-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2}}\mathcal{A}^{+}(h\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2}})\right],

which results in the same integral as equation (
III.5
).

III.1.3
The generalized Stieltjes transform

The last integral in equation (
III.5
) is also consistent with the general inversion formula of the GST
(
Schwarz 2005
)
:

f(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{g(t)\,\mathrm{d}t}{(t+x)^{1+\eta}}

(III.6)

which admits the inversion via a contour integral: if
\eta>0
,

g(t)=\frac{\eta}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\!\int\limits_{-t}^{(0+)}\mathrm{d}z\,(t+z)^{\eta-1}f(z).

(III.7)

Here we have used the notation of
Whittaker & Watson 1927
for the path of integration; that is, the contour starts and ends at
z=-t
(on the opposite side of the branch cut) while it encircles the point
z=0
in the positive sense.

Since equation (
III.2
) is the GST of order
1+\eta=\frac{3}{2}
, applying the integral inversion formula (i.e. eq.
III.7
) results in

\Lambda(h)=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}}\!\int\limits_{-h^{2}}^{(0+)}\frac{\Sigma(\rho^{1/2})\,\mathrm{d}\rho}{\!\sqrt{\rho+h^{2}}}=\frac{2}{\mathrm{i}}\!\int\limits_{-\mathrm{i}h}^{\mathrm{i}h}\!\frac{R\Sigma(R)\,\mathrm{d}R}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+h^{2}}},

which recovers the same formula as equation (
III.5
). Here the integration path of the last
R
-integral starts at
R=-\mathrm{i}h
, cuts (upward) the positive half of the real-
R
axis, and ends at
R=\mathrm{i}h
. Thanks to the Cauchy theorem, the specific choice of the integration path is immaterial, provided that
\Sigma(R)
is extended analytically to the right half-space of the complex number (i.e.
R\in\mathbb{C}
and
\Re[R]>0
). In other words, if the
analytic continuation
of
\Sigma(R)
or
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
is known for the
complex
domain, the
1
weight, provided that it actually exists, can be recovered via a complex quadrature.

For instance, if
\Sigma(R)=C(R^{2}+a^{2})^{-p}
, then (here
R=\mathrm{i}y
)

\frac{\Lambda(h)}{C}=2\mathrm{i}\!\int_{-h}^{h}\!\frac{y\,\mathrm{d}y}{(a^{2}-y^{2})^{p}\!\sqrt{h^{2}-y^{2}}}.

(III.8)

This vanishes if
a>h>0
, for the integrand with
y\in[-h,h]
is a real-valued even function of
y
. If
h>a>0
on the other hand, the integrand has the branch points at
y=\pm a\in[-h,h]
. With proper branch cuts, equation (
III.8
) for
h>a>0
is reducible to

\begin{split}\frac{\Lambda(h)}{C}&=\int_{a}^{h}\!\frac{4\sin(p\pi)\,y\,\mathrm{d}y}{(y^{2}-a^{2})^{p}\!\sqrt{h^{2}-y^{2}}}\\
&=\frac{2\pi^{3/2}(h^{2}-a^{2})^{1/2-p}}{\Gamma(p)\Gamma(3/2-p)},\end{split}

(III.9)

where
\Gamma(x)
is the Gamma function. That is to say, if we use
\Lambda\propto(h^{2}-a^{2})^{1/2-p}\Theta(h^{2}-a^{2})
– where
\Theta(x)
is the Heaviside unit step – as the weight, the GST in equation (
III.2
) then results in
\Sigma\propto(R^{2}+a^{2})^{-p}
(for
0<p<\frac{3}{2}
), which can be confirmed through direct calculations. See Sect.
V
for more details of this family of the models.

IV
The Mellin transform approach

While the inversion formula of equation (
III.5
) completes the formal machinery to apply the method of
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
, its actual utility is still limited. This is not only because the inversion requires the analytic continuation in the complex domain but also because an arbitrary surface density is not necessarily the GST of a proper non-negative weight. In fact, the surface density
\Sigma(R)
in equation (
III.2
) with any non-negative
\Lambda(h)
cannot decay faster than
R^{-3}
as
R\to\infty
(in particular, if
\int_{I}\mathrm{d}hh\Lambda(h)
converges, then
\Sigma\sim R^{-3}
). In other words, any surface density decaying faster than
R^{-3}
(including all finite disks) cannot be expressed as the GST in equation (
III.2
) with a proper weight.

Nevertheless we can still make use of equations (
III.2
) to find the rotation curve generated by any circularly symmetric disk. Instead of inserting equation (
III.5
) directly to equation (
III.2
) to get the transformation
\Sigma\to\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
, let us consider the Mellin transforms
R^{2}\to t

(
Erdélyi et al. 1954
, chap. VI)
first;

\begin{split}\mathfrak{M}[\Sigma](t)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}\rho\,\rho^{t-1}\Sigma(\!\sqrt{\rho})=2\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}R\,R^{2t-1}\Sigma(R);\\
\mathfrak{M}[\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}](t)&=2\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}R\,R^{2t-1}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R).\end{split}

(IV.1)

Replacing
\Sigma
and
\varv_{c}^{2}
with equation (
III.2
) leads these to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{M}[\Sigma](t)&=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}h\,h\Lambda(h)\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{R^{2t-1}\mathrm{d}R}{(R^{2}+h^{2})^{3/2}}\\
&=\frac{\Gamma(t)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-t)}{\pi^{3/2}}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}h\,h^{2t-2}\Lambda(h);\end{split}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}[\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}](t)

\displaystyle=\frac{2G\Gamma(t+1)\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-t)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}h\,h^{2t-1}\Lambda(h).

The integrals in the right-hand sides are recognized as the Mellin transform of
\Lambda
and related to
\mathfrak{M}[\Sigma](t)
and
\mathfrak{M}[\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}](t)
:

\begin{split}\mathfrak{M}[\Lambda](t)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\,\eta^{t-1}\Lambda(\!\sqrt{\eta})=2\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}h\,h^{2t-1}\Lambda(h)\\
&=\frac{2\pi^{3/2}\mathfrak{M}[\Sigma](t+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(t+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(1-t)}=\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\mathfrak{M}[\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}](t)}{G\Gamma(t+1)\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-t)}.\end{split}

(IV.2)

Utilizing the Mellin inversion theorem,

g(t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}x\,x^{t-1}f(x)\ \Rightarrow\ f(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int\limits_{\gamma-\mathrm{i}\infty}^{\gamma+\mathrm{i}\infty}\frac{g(t)}{x^{t}}\mathrm{d}t,

(IV.3)

the weight
\Lambda(h)
is in principle recoverable as in

\begin{split}\Lambda(h)&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\!\int\limits_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{h^{2t}}\frac{2\pi^{3/2}\mathfrak{M}[\Sigma](t+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(t+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(1-t)}\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int\limits_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{h^{2t}}\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\mathfrak{M}[\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}](t)}{G\Gamma(t+1)\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-t)}.\end{split}

(IV.4)

Here the specific integration path of equation (
IV.3
)
is replaced with a generic complex contour
\mathcal{C}
that includes
the point at infinity, which is
permissible provided the path deformation does not encounter
any poles or branch cuts of the integrand.
Equation (
IV.2
) also directly relates
\mathfrak{M}[\Sigma](t)

and
\mathfrak{M}[\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}](t)
to each other,
resulting in the formulae;

\begin{split}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)&=\frac{2\pi G}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\!\int\limits_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{R^{2t}}\frac{\Gamma(t+1)\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-t)}{\Gamma(t+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(1-t)}\mathfrak{M}[\Sigma](t+\tfrac{1}{2});\\
\Sigma(R)&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\!\int\limits_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{R^{2t}}\frac{\Gamma(t)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-t)}{\Gamma(t+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(1-t)}\frac{\mathfrak{M}[\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}](t-\tfrac{1}{2})}{2\pi G}.\end{split}

(IV.5)

These are valid in general even when a proper weight
\Lambda
does not exist. In principle, one can derive a similar formula for
\Phi
via the double Mellin transform of equation (
III.1
) (see e.g.,
An 2026
, eq. II.8). However, since
\Phi
is a function of two variables
(R,z)
, the result involves a double integral expression, which is less practical. Although these formulae appear rather too formal and to be of little practical use, their true value actually lies with ubiquity of the Mellin–Barnes integral representations for many classes of special functions of interest. In particular, the Meijer-G functions recently have become more widely accepted in many applications, thanks in part to their ready availability in various computer algebra packages.

Suppose that
\Sigma(R)
is representable as the Meijer-G function (Appendix
B
; see also
Erdélyi et al. 1953
, chap. 5.3 or
Olver et al. 2010
, § 16.17) as in

\Sigma(R)=\Sigma_{0}\,G^{m,n}_{p,q}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a_1,\cdots,a_p}{b_1,\dots,b_q}\right),

(IV.6)

with constants
\Sigma_{0}
and
R_{0}
. By definition,

\mathfrak{M}[\Sigma](t)=\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{2t}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Gamma(1-a_{i}-t)}{\prod_{i=n+1}^{p}\Gamma(t+a_{i})}\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Gamma(t+b_{j})}{\prod_{j=m+1}^{q}\Gamma(1-b_{j}-t)},

and so follows equations (
IV.4
) and (
IV.5
) that both
\Lambda(h)
and
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
are also expressible as the Meijer-G function,

\begin{split}\frac{\Lambda(h)}{2\pi^{3/2}\Sigma_{0}R_{0}}&=G^{m,n}_{p+1,q+1}\!\left(\frac{h^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a_1+\frac 12,\cdots,a_p+\frac 12,\frac 12}{b_1+\frac 12,\dots,b_q+\frac 12,0}\right);\\
\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}R_{0}}&=G^{m+1,n+1}_{p+2,q+2}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,a_1+\frac 12,\cdots,a_p+\frac 12,\frac 12}{1,b_1+\frac 12,\dots,b_q+\frac 12,0}\right).\end{split}

(IV.7)

Similarly if the rotation curve is expressible as

\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)=\varv_{0}^{2}\,G^{m,n}_{p,q}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a_1,\cdots,a_p}{b_1,\dots,b_q}\right),

(IV.8)

then
\Lambda(h)
and
\Sigma(R)
are given by

\begin{split}\Lambda(h)&=\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\varv_{0}^{2}}{G}G^{m,n}_{p+1,q+1}\!\left(\frac{h^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a_1,\cdots,a_p,1}{b_1,\dots,b_q,\frac 12}\right);\\
\Sigma(R)&=\frac{\varv_{0}^{2}}{2\pi GR_{0}}G^{m+1,n+1}_{p+2,q+2}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-\frac 12,a_1-\frac 12,\cdots,a_p-\frac 12,\frac 12}{0,b_1-\frac 12,\dots,b_q-\frac 12,0}\right).\end{split}

Here both transformations
\Sigma\to\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
and
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\to\Sigma
appear to complicate the result, for it sends
G^{m,n}_{p,q}
to
G^{m+1,n+1}_{p+2,q+2}
in general. However, if the gamma functions in the Mellin transforms of equation (
IV.5
) cancel another factors in the transformation kernel, the results can be simplified.

For completeness, we also consider the forward transformation of
\Lambda(h)
given by the Meijer-G function;

\Lambda(h)=\Lambda_{0}\,G^{m,n}_{p,q}\!\left(\frac{h^{2}}{h_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a_1,\cdots,a_p}{b_1,\dots,b_q}\right),

(IV.9)

which then results in

\displaystyle\Sigma(R)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{2\pi^{3/2}h_{0}}G^{m+1,n+1}_{p+1,q+1}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{h_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-\frac 12,a_1-\frac 12,\cdots,a_p-\frac 12}{0,b_1-\frac 12,\dots,b_q-\frac 12}\right);

(IV.10)

\displaystyle\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)

\displaystyle=\frac{G\Lambda_{0}}{\!\sqrt{\pi}}G^{m+1,n+1}_{p+1,q+1}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{h_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,a_1,\cdots,a_p}{1,b_1,\dots,b_q}\right).

(IV.11)

This is a straightforward consequence of the Meijer-G convolution theorem since equation (
III.2
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\begin{split}2\pi^{\frac{3}{2}}\Sigma(R)&=\frac{1}{R}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}(h^{2})}{h^{2}}G^{1,1}_{1,1}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{h^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{0}{\frac 12}\right)\Lambda(h)\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}(h^{2})}{h^{2}}G^{1,1}_{1,1}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{h^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-\frac 12}{0}\right)\frac{\Lambda(h)}{h};\end{split}

\displaystyle\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}}{G}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}(h^{2})}{h^{2}}G^{1,1}_{1,1}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{h^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12}{1}\right)\Lambda(h).

IV.1
The potential on the symmetry axis

Under the axial symmetry, the potential at a point on the symmetry axis is written down as a single integral:

\Phi(0,z)=-2\pi G\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{R\Sigma(R)\,\mathrm{d}R}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+z^{2}}}.

(IV.12)

However this converges only if

1
1

1

In fact,
\lim_{R\to 0}R^{2}\Sigma(R)=0
is also necessary, but this must hold for any integrable surface density.

\lim_{R\to\infty}R\Sigma(R)=0
(i.e.
p_{\infty}>1
where
\Sigma\sim R^{-p_{\infty}}
as
R\to\infty
), reflecting the condition for the zero-point to be set at infinity. On the other hand, following a similar trick for equation (
II.5
), we may obtain an alternative integral for the potential with the zero-point at the origin:

\Phi(0,z)=2\pi G\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}R\,\Sigma(R)\frac{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+z^{2}}-R}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+z^{2}}},

which converges if
\lim_{R\to\infty}R^{-1}\Sigma(R)=\lim_{R\to 0}R\Sigma(R)=0
(i.e.
p_{\infty}>-1
and
p_{0}<1
where
\Sigma\sim R^{-p_{0}}
as
R\to 0
).

Equation (
IV.12
) is also recognized as a GST of order
1/2
. Hence it is also possible to invert
\Phi(0,z)
for
\Sigma(R)
based on the techniques discussed in Sect.
III.1
. However given ambiguity of the zero-point, it is more appropriate to consider inverting the gravitational acceleration instead. The gravitational acceleration on the symmetry axis due to the disk as a function of the height is

\displaystyle\varg(z)

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\frac{\mathrm{d}\Phi(0,z)}{\mathrm{d}z}\right\rvert=2\pi Gz\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{R\Sigma(R)\,\mathrm{d}R}{(R^{2}+z^{2})^{3/2}}

(IV.13)

\displaystyle=2\pi G\left[\Sigma(0)+z\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}R}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+z^{2}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\Sigma(R)}{\mathrm{d}R}\right]\hskip 10.00002pt(z>0)

which converges assuming
\lim_{R\to\infty}\Sigma(R)/R=0
. If the total disk mass,
M_{\mathrm{t}}=2\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}R\,R\Sigma(R)
is finite, the acceleration decays like
\varg(z)\simeq GM_{\mathrm{t}}/z^{2}
as
z\to\infty
, but the gravitational force due to an infinite-mass disk can fall off slower.

Equation (
IV.13
) is formally equivalent to equation (
III.2
), and so the inversion formula for
\varg(z)\to\Sigma(R)
follows that for
R\Sigma(R)\to\Lambda(h)
. If the analytic continuation of
\varg(z)
for the complex right half-plane (i.e.
\Re[z]>0
and
|\arg(z)|<\pi/2
) is available, the integral inversion formula is given by

\pi G\Sigma(R)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int\limits_{-\mathrm{i}R}^{\mathrm{i}R}\frac{\varg(z)\,\mathrm{d}z}{\!\sqrt{z^{2}+R^{2}}},

which is analogous to equations (
III.5
). Alternatively by considering the Mellin transform of
\varg(z)
:

\begin{split}\mathfrak{M}[\varg](t)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}(z^{2})\,z^{2(t-1)}\varg(z)=2\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}z\,z^{2t-1}\varg(z)\\
&=4\!\sqrt{\pi}\,G\Gamma(t+\tfrac{1}{2})\Gamma(1-t)\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}R\,R^{2t-1}\Sigma(R),\end{split}

we also derive an inversion formula similar to equation (
IV.4
);

2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,G\,\Sigma(R)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int\limits_{\mathcal{C}}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{R^{2t}}\frac{\mathfrak{M}[\varg](t)}{\Gamma(t+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(1-t)}.

Moreover, together with equation (
IV.2
), we can establish that
\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\mathfrak{M}[\varg](t)=\Gamma(t)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-t)\mathfrak{M}[\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}](t-\frac{1}{2})
, and so follows that

\begin{split}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)&=\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int\limits_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{R^{2t}}\frac{\mathfrak{M}[\varg](t+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(t+\frac{1}{2})(1-t)};\\
\varg(z)&=\frac{2}{\!\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int\limits_{\mathcal{C}}\!\mathrm{d}t\,\frac{\Gamma(t)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-t)}{z^{2t}}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}R\,R^{2t-2}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)\\
&=\frac{2}{\!\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}R\,\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{R^{2}}\,G^{1,1}_{1,1}\!\left(\frac{z^{2}}{R^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-\frac 12}{0}\right)\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{R\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)\,\mathrm{d}R}{(R^{2}+z^{2})^{3/2}}=\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(0)}{z}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}R}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+z^{2}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{\mathrm{d}R}.\end{split}

Therefore the disk with the surface density of equation (
IV.6
) produces the vertical acceleration profile of the form

\varg(z)=2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,G\Sigma_{0}\,G^{m+1,n+1}_{p+1,q+1}\!\left(\frac{z^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{0,a_1,\dotsc,a_p}{\frac 12,b_1,\dotsc,b_q}\right),

whereas the vertical acceleration due to the disk generating rotation curve of equation (
IV.8
) is found to be

\varg(z)=\frac{\varv_{0}^{2}}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,R_{0}}G^{m+1,n+1}_{p+1,q+1}\!\left(\frac{z^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-\frac 12,a_1-\frac 12,\dotsc,a_p-\frac 12}{0,b_1-\frac 12,\dotsc,b_q-\frac 12}\right).

Conversely if the vertical acceleration profile is known to be

\varg(z)=\varg_{0}\,G^{m,n}_{p,q}\left(\frac{z^{2}}{z_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a_1,\dotsc,a_p}{b_1,\dotsc,b_q}\right),

then the disk surface density and the rotation curve should be

\begin{split}\Sigma(R)&=\frac{\varg_{0}}{2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,G}G^{m,n}_{p+1,q+1}\left(\frac{R^{2}}{z_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a_1,\dotsc,a_p,\frac 12}{b_1,\dotsc,b_q,0}\right);\\
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)&=\!\sqrt{\pi}\,z_{0}\varg_{0}\,G^{m,n}_{p+1,q+1}\left(\frac{R^{2}}{z_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a_1+\frac 12,\dotsc,a_p+\frac 12,\frac 12}{b_1+\frac 12,\dotsc,b_q+\frac 12,0}\right).\end{split}

IV.2
The Qian Hypergeometric disks

Note that the generalized hypergeometric function is a special case of the Meijer-G functions (cf. eq
B.2
): that is,

x^{2t}{{}_{p}F_{q}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a_1,\dotsc,a_p}{b_1,\dotsc,b_q};-x^{2}\right)=\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{q}\Gamma(b_{j})}{\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Gamma(a_{i})}\\
\times G^{1,p}_{p,q+1}\!\left(x^{2}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1-a_1+t,\dotsc,1-a_p+t}{t,1-b_1+t,\dotsc,1-b_q+t}\right).

(IV.14)

Hence the disk with the surface density expressible to be

\frac{\Sigma(R)}{\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Gamma(a_{i})}{\prod_{j=1}^{q}\Gamma(b_{j})}{{}_{p}F_{q}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a_1,\dotsc,a_p}{b_1,\dotsc,b_q};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right)

(IV.15)

generates the mid-plane rotation curve behaving like

\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{G\Sigma_{0}R_{0}}=\frac{\pi^{3/2}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Gamma(a_{i}+\frac{1}{2})}{\prod_{j=1}^{q}\Gamma(b_{j}+\frac{1}{2})}\\
\times\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\,{{}_{p+1}F_{q+1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,a_1+\frac 12,\dotsc,a_p+\frac 12}{2,b_1+\frac 12,\dotsc,b_q+\frac 12};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right),

(IV.16)

while the mid-plane potential is recoverable through integrating
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=R[\mathrm{d}\Phi(R,0)/\mathrm{d}R]=2x(\mathrm{d}\Phi/\mathrm{d}x)
(where
x=R^{2}/R_{0}^{2}
),

\frac{\Phi(R,0)}{G\Sigma_{0}R_{0}}=-\frac{\pi^{3/2}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Gamma(a_{i}-\frac{1}{2})}{\prod_{j=1}^{q}\Gamma(b_{j}-\frac{1}{2})}\\
\times{{}_{p+1}F_{q+1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,a_1-\frac 12,\dotsc,a_p-\frac 12}{1,b_1-\frac 12,\dotsc,b_q-\frac 12};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right).

Equations (
IV.15
) and (
IV.16
) reproduce equations (3.3) and (3.5) of
Qian 1992
for the axisymmetric case (
m=0
), and so the formulae utilizing the Meijer-G function supersede those of
Qian 1992
based on the generalized hypergeometric functions. In principle, the modifications to include the non-axisymmetric cases are possible following similar procedures as
Qian 1992
establishing the integral transforms for the higher-order Fourier components although we shall not pursue this approach in this paper.

The vertical acceleration due to the disk of the same surface density and the weight function resulting in the same disk are both formally given by the Meijer-G function:

\displaystyle\frac{\varg(z)}{G\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,G^{2,p+1}_{p+1,q+2}\!\left(\frac{z^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{0,1-a_1,\dotsc,1-a_p}{0,\frac 12,1-b_1,\dotsc,1-b_p}\right);

\displaystyle\frac{\Lambda(h)}{\Sigma_{0}R_{0}}

\displaystyle=2\pi^{\frac{3}{2}}\,G^{0,p}_{p,q+1}\!\left(\frac{h^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32-a_1,\cdots,\frac 32-a_p}{\frac 32-b_1,\dots,\frac 32-b_q,0}\right).

(IV.17)

Although equation (
IV.14
) is not applicable here, both of these Meijer-G functions are reducible to the hypergeometric series;
{{}_{p+1}F_{q+1}}
for
\varg(z)
and
{{}_{p}F_{q}}
for
\Lambda(h)
. For instance,
\varg(z)
reduces to the hypergeometric series of the form (here
\zeta=z/R_{0}
)

\frac{\varg(z)}{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(1+k/2)\Gamma(a_{i}+k/2)}{\Gamma(b_{j}+k/2)}\frac{(-2\zeta)^{k}}{k!}\\
=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Gamma(a_{i})}{\prod_{j=1}^{q}\Gamma(b_{j})}{{}_{p+1}F_{q+1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,a_1,\dotsc,a_p}{\frac 12,b_1,\dotsc,b_q};\zeta^{2}\right)\\
-\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Gamma(a_{i}+\frac{1}{2})}{\prod_{j=1}^{q}\Gamma(b_{j}+\frac{1}{2})}\zeta{{}_{p}F_{q}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a_1+\frac 12,\dotsc,a_p+\frac 12}{b_1+\frac 12,\dotsc,b_q+\frac 12};\zeta^{2}\right),

With
\Lambda
found, the potentials due to these disks follow equation (
III.1
), which may be evaluated as a real quadrature. In the subsequent sections, we shall consider when this integral can be resolved by analytic means. Following
Qian 1992
, we restrict ourselves to the cases that both
\Sigma(R)
and
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
are expressible through at most a single
{{}_{2}F_{1}}
-function.

Before exploring this, we finish this section by considering the disk generating the rotation curve given in the form of

\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{\varv_{0}^{2}}=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Gamma(a_{i})}{\prod_{j=1}^{q}\Gamma(b_{j})}\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\,{{}_{p}F_{q}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a_1,\dotsc,a_p}{b_1,\dotsc,b_q};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right).

The surface density of this razor-thin disk is then found to be

\Sigma(R)=\frac{\varv_{0}^{2}}{GR_{0}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Gamma(a_{i}-\frac{1}{2})}{4\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\prod_{j=1}^{q}\Gamma(b_{j}-\frac{1}{2})}\\
\times{{}_{p+1}F_{q+1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,a_1-\frac 12,\dotsc,a_p-\frac 12}{1,b_1-\frac 12,\dotsc,b_q-\frac 12};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right),

(IV.18)

while the vertical acceleration along the axis is given by

\begin{split}\varg(z)&=\frac{\varv_{0}^{2}}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,R_{0}}G^{2,p+1}_{p+1,q+2}\!\left(\frac{z^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-\frac 12,\frac 32-a_1,\dotsc,\frac 32-a_p}{0,\frac 12,\frac 32-b_1,\dotsc,\frac 32-b_n}\right)\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}+k/2)\Gamma(a_{i}-\frac{1}{2}+k/2)}{k!\Gamma(b_{j}-\frac{1}{2}+k/2)}\left(-\frac{2z}{R_{0}}\right)^{k}\\
&=\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Gamma(a_{i}-\frac{1}{2})}{2\prod_{j=1}^{q}\Gamma(b_{j}-\frac{1}{2})}{{}_{p+1}F_{q+1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,a_i-\frac 12}{\frac 12,b_j-\frac 12};\frac{z^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right)\\
&\qquad-\frac{2\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Gamma(a_{i})}{\prod_{j=1}^{q}\Gamma(b_{j})}\zeta\,{{}_{p+1}F_{q+1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{2,a_i}{\frac 32,b_j};\frac{z^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right).\end{split}

Finally the weight
\Lambda(h)
corresponding to the disk with the surface density of equation (
IV.18
) is

\Lambda(h)=\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\varv_{0}^{2}}{G}\,G^{0,p}_{p,q+1}\!\left(\frac{h^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{2-a_1,\cdots,2-a_p}{2-b_1,\dots,2-b_q,\frac 12}\right).

V
The Kuzmin–Mestel–Toomre disks

As the simplest example, we first consider the disk generated by the
1
weight in the form of equation (
III.9
). In particular, let us consider the disk with the surface density of

\Sigma(R)=\Sigma_{0}\left(1+\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right)^{-\gamma}=\frac{\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{2\gamma}}{(R^{2}+R_{0}^{2})^{\gamma}}.

(V.1)

Here
\Sigma_{0}
and
R_{0}
are the scale constants and
a
controls the shape. The surface density at center is finite,
\Sigma(0)=\Sigma_{0}
whereas
\Sigma\sim R^{-2\gamma}
as
R\to\infty
with the
\gamma=0
case corresponding to an infinite uniform plate. If
\gamma>1
, the mass contained in the disk increases with the radius like
M(R)/M_{\mathrm{t}}=1-(1+\varrho^{2})^{-(\gamma-1)}
, where
\varrho=R_{0}/R
and
M_{\mathrm{t}}=\pi R_{0}^{2}\Sigma_{0}/(\gamma-1)
is the total mass of the disk. If
\gamma\leq 1
on the other hand, the mass grows without any bound: in particular,
M(R)/(\pi R_{0}^{2}\Sigma)=\ln(1+\varrho^{2})
for
\gamma=1
and
M(R)/(\pi R_{0}^{2}\Sigma)=[(1+\varrho^{2})^{1-\gamma}-1]/(1-\gamma)
for
\gamma<1
. If
\gamma-\frac{1}{2}=n
is a positive integer, the model is the same as the Model-
n
of
Toomre 1963
, while the
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
and
\frac{3}{2}
cases are respectively identified with the cored
Mestel 1963
disks and the
Kuzmin 1956
disks. Hereafter we shall refer to this family as the KMTd (after
Kuzmin 1956
–
Mestel 1963
–
Toomre 1963
disk).

Given that the Mellin transform (
R^{2}\to t
as defined in eq.
IV.1
) of equation (
V.1
) is found to be

\mathfrak{M}[\Sigma](t)=R_{0}^{2t}\Sigma_{0}\frac{\Gamma(t)\Gamma(\gamma-t)}{\Gamma(\gamma)}\hskip 10.00002pt(0<t<\gamma),

the surface density of equation (
V.1
) is also expressed as

\frac{\Sigma(R)}{\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\gamma)}G^{1,1}_{1,1}\!\left(\varrho^{2}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1-\gamma}{0}\right)={{}_{1}F_{0}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\gamma}{};-\varrho^{2}\right).

where again
\varrho\equiv R/R_{0}
(and henceforth). Therefore the rotation curve due to the KMTd is

\begin{split}\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}&=\frac{2\pi}{\Gamma(\gamma)}G^{1,2}_{2,2}\!\left(\varrho^{2}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,\frac 32-\gamma}{1,0}\right)\\
&=\frac{\pi^{3/2}\Gamma(\gamma+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\gamma)}\varrho^{2}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,\gamma+\frac 12}{2};-\varrho^{2}\right);\end{split}

(V.2)

for
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}
, whereas the vertical acceleration is

\displaystyle\frac{\varg(z)}{G\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\!\sqrt{\pi}}{\Gamma(\gamma)}\,G^{2,2}_{2,2}\!\left(\frac{z^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{0,1-\gamma}{0,\frac 12}\right)

\displaystyle=2\pi\left[{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,\gamma}{\frac 12};\zeta^{2}\right)-\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\gamma+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\gamma)}\frac{\zeta}{(1-\zeta^{2})^{\gamma+1/2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{2\gamma+1}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\gamma,1}{\gamma+\frac 32};1-\zeta^{2}\right),

(V.3)

where
\zeta\equiv z/R_{0}
and henceforth. While the last line follows Kummer’s analytic continuation formula for the Gauss-
{{}_{2}F_{1}}
hypergeometric functions (see
Erdélyi et al. 1953
, eq. 2.10.(1)), it can be also derived directly from equation (
IV.13
). If
\gamma=0
, then
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=0
and
\varg=2\pi G\Sigma_{0}
, which are consistent with the translation symmetry implying no net gravitational force within the infinite uniform plate and the scale invariance leading to the constant force off the plate. For
-\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<0
, we find
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}<0
, which reflects the fact that the gravitational force directs radially outward in the corresponding cases. If
\gamma\leq-\frac{1}{2}
, the gravity from matters at infinity dominates and the integral for the gravitational force diverges. Although a formal treatment is still possible, an infinite disk is an artificial construction in any case and so this is not a physical problem.

Equations (
V.2
) and (
V.3
) indicate that the rotation curve and the vertical acceleration as
r\to\infty
behave like
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\simeq GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R
and
\varg\simeq GM_{\mathrm{t}}/z^{2}
for finite-mass disks (
\gamma>1
), or

\begin{split}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)&\simeq 2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}\times\begin{cases}\varrho^{-1}[\ln(4\varrho)-1]&(\gamma=1)\\
A_{\gamma}\,\varrho^{1-2\gamma}&(1>\gamma>-\frac{1}{2})\end{cases},\\
\varg(z)&\simeq 2\pi G\Sigma_{0}\times\begin{cases}\zeta^{-2}[\ln(2\zeta)-1]&(\gamma=1)\\
B_{\gamma}\,\zeta^{-2\gamma}&(1>\gamma>-\frac{1}{2})\end{cases};\\
A_{\gamma}&=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\gamma)\Gamma(1-\gamma)}{\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)},\quad B_{\gamma}=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\gamma)\Gamma(1-\gamma)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}}.\end{split}

Here
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}(R)
for
\gamma<\frac{1}{2}
diverges as
R\to\infty
and the corresponding potential also diverges like
\Phi\sim r^{2\gamma-1}
asymptotically (note
\varg\sim z^{-2\gamma}
). On the other hand, the rotation curve due to the
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
case (the cored Mestel disk) is asymptotically flat and the potential diverges logarithmically.

The proper
1
weight function of the KMTd exists for
0<\gamma<\frac{3}{2}
and is formally given by (cf. eq.
III.9
)

\begin{split}\Lambda(h)&=\frac{2\pi^{3/2}R_{0}\Sigma_{0}}{\Gamma(\gamma)}G^{0,1}_{1,1}\!\left(\frac{h^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32-\gamma}{0}\right)\\
&=\frac{2\pi^{3/2}R_{0}^{2\gamma}\Sigma_{0}}{\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)}\frac{\Theta(h^{2}-R_{0}^{2})}{(h^{2}-R_{0}^{2})^{\gamma-1/2}}\end{split}

(V.4)

where
\Theta(x)
is the Heaviside unit step. Hence the full potential due to the KMTd can be written down as

\Phi(R,z)=-\frac{2\pi^{3/2}GR_{0}^{2\gamma}\Sigma_{0}}{\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)}\int_{R_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{(h^{2}-R_{0}^{2})^{1/2-\gamma}\mathrm{d}h}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+h)^{2}}},

(V.5)

which converges for
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<\frac{3}{2}
. The non-convergence for
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{2}
is due to the impossibility of setting the zero-point for the potential at infinity. If the alternative zero-point
\Phi(0,0)=0
is chosen instead, the adjustment similar to equation (
II.5
) leads to the zero-point-shifted potential in a quadrature;

\Phi(R,z)=-\frac{2\pi^{3/2}GR_{0}^{2\gamma}\Sigma_{0}}{\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)}\\
\times\int_{R_{0}}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}h\,(h^{2}-R_{0}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma}\left[\frac{1}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+h)^{2}}}-\frac{1}{h}\right],

(V.6)

which is convergent if
0<\gamma<\frac{3}{2}
. Note that equation (
V.6
) with
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
recovers the potential of the cored Mestel disk in equation (
II.9
) with
\varv_{\infty}^{2}=2\pi G\Sigma_{0}R_{0}
and
a=c=R_{0}
.

With either integral in equation (
V.5
) or (
V.6
), one can also derive the expression for the mid-plane rotation curve, which recovers equation (
V.2
). On the other hand, the vertical acceleration follows the potential as

\begin{split}\varg&=\frac{2\pi^{3/2}GR_{0}^{2\gamma}\Sigma_{0}}{\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(h+2R_{0})^{1/2-\gamma}h^{1/2-\gamma}\mathrm{d}h}{(z+R_{0}+h)^{2}}\\
&=\frac{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}}{2\gamma+1}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{2\gamma,2}{\gamma+\frac 32};\frac{1-\zeta}{2}\right),\end{split}

(V.7)

which is equivalent to equation (
V.3
) thanks to the quadratic transformations for the
{{}_{2}F_{1}}
-functions (e.g.,
Olver et al. 2010
, eq. 15.8.18). Although the integral in equation (
V.7
) converges only if
0<\gamma<\frac{3}{2}
, the last expression is valid for any
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}
.

If
\gamma\geq\frac{3}{2}
or
\gamma\leq 0
, equation (
III.2
) with
\Lambda
given by equation (
V.4
) diverges. The proper weight for
\gamma=\frac{3}{2}
is instead
\Lambda=2\pi R_{0}^{2}\Sigma_{0}\deltaup(h-R_{0})=4\pi R_{0}^{3}\Sigma_{0}\deltaup(h^{2}-R_{0}^{2})
where
\deltaup(x)
is the Dirac delta. This is equivalent to identifying with
\lim_{\xi\downarrow 0^{+}}[(x-y)^{\xi-1}/\Gamma(\xi)]=\deltaup(x-y)
in equation (
V.4
). Equation (
III.1
) with the Dirac delta weight then recovers the potential of the Kuzmin disk (with
a=R_{0}
and
M_{\mathrm{t}}=2\pi\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{2}
) in equation (
II.12
). For
\gamma>\frac{3}{2}
however, a proper non-negative weight does not exist because, as noted earlier in Sect.
IV
, any surface density expressible in the form of equation (
III.2
) with
\Lambda\geq 0
cannot decay faster than
\sim R^{-3}
asymptotically.

V.1
The recursion relation and the Weyl integrals

None the less, the potential due to the KMTd for
\gamma>\frac{3}{2}
is still found by following
Toomre 1963
. In particular, observe that equation (
V.1
) obeys the recursion relation:

\frac{\partial}{\partial(R_{0}^{2})}\frac{\Sigma(R;\gamma)}{\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{2\gamma}}=-\gamma\frac{\Sigma(R;\gamma+1)}{\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{2(\gamma+1)}}.

(V.8)

The linearity of the Poisson equation indicates that the potential due to the disk also follows the analogous relation that

\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial(R_{0}^{2})}\right]^{m}\frac{\Phi(\bm{r};\gamma)}{\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{2\gamma}}=(-1)^{m}(\gamma)_{m}\frac{\Phi(\bm{r};\gamma+m)}{\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{2(\gamma+m)}},

(V.9)

where
(a)_{n}=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(a+j)=\Gamma(a+n)/\Gamma(a)
is the Pochhammer symbol (rising sequential product). That is, if
m=\lceil\gamma-\frac{3}{2}\rceil
is the integer ceiling of
\gamma-\frac{3}{2}
, the potential due to the KMTd is obtained from the
m
-th derivative of equation (
V.5
). In particular, the potential for
\gamma=\frac{3}{2}+m
with a non-negative integer
m
– corresponding to the
Toomre 1963
Model-
(m+1)
– is given by

\Phi(R,z)=-\frac{\pi GR_{0}^{3+2m}\Sigma_{0}}{(-1)^{m}(\frac{1}{2})_{m+1}}\frac{\partial^{m}}{\partial(R_{0}^{2})^{m}}\frac{1}{R_{0}\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+R_{0})^{2}}}\\
=-\frac{\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}{(\frac{1}{2})_{m+1}}\frac{\partial^{m}}{\partial x^{m}}\frac{x^{m}}{\sqrt{(1+x^{1/2}|z|)^{2}+xR^{2}}}\biggr\rvert_{x=R_{0}^{-2}}.

(V.10)

Here the second line follows theorem A3 of
An 2011
. With a formal identification of
(\partial/\partial x)^{-1}=\int\!\mathrm{d}x
, equation (
V.10
) may also be extended to
m=-1
(i.e.
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
): namely,

\Phi(R,z)=2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}R_{0}}{\!\sqrt{(R_{0}+|z|)^{2}+R^{2}}}\\
=2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}\ln\Bigl[R_{0}+|z|+\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+R_{0})^{2}}\Bigr]+C,

(V.11)

which is equivalent to equation (
V.6
) with
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
. In other words, the cored Mestel disk is recognized as the
Toomre 1963
Model-0 disk
(
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
)
.

The general expression of the potential for the KMTd with
\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
can be written down compactly by introducing the Weyl integrals
(
Erdélyi et al. 1954
, chap. XIII)
:

{{}_{b}^{-}I}_{x}^{\eta}\,f(x)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma(\eta)}\int_{x}^{b}\!(y-x)^{\eta-1}f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y&(\eta>0)\\
f(x)&(\eta=0)\end{cases},

(V.12)

for
\eta\geq 0
, and, if
\eta=-\xi<0
, then

{{}_{b}^{-}I}_{x}^{-\xi}\,f(x)=\biggl(-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\biggr)^{\lfloor\xi\rfloor+1}{{}_{b}^{-}I}_{x}^{1-\{\xi\}}f=\biggl(-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\biggr)^{\lceil\xi\rceil}{{}_{b}^{-}I}_{x}^{\lceil\xi\rceil-\xi}f.

Here
\lfloor\xi\rfloor
,
\lceil\xi\rceil
and
\{\xi\}=\xi-\lfloor\xi\rfloor
are the integer floor, the integer ceiling, and the fractional part of
\xi>0
respectively. If
m
is a non-negative integer,
{{}_{b}^{-}I}_{x}^{-m}f(x)=(-1)^{m}f^{(m)}(x)
, and thus the Weyl integral may be considered as a generalization of differentiations. The Weyl integral for a complex variable is equivalent to the Riemann–Liouville fractional integral (see e.g.,
An et al. 2012
), and the explicit transformations of Weyl’s to the Riemann–Liouville integral restricted for a real positive variable also exist (e.g., change of the integration variable
y\to y^{-1}
). Finally note the composite rule
{{}_{b}^{-}I}_{x}^{\xi}\,{{}_{b}^{-}I}_{x}^{\eta}f={{}_{b}^{-}I}_{x}^{\xi+\eta}f
, which is valid for
\eta>-1
and any
\xi
. Then combining equations (
V.5
) and (
V.9
) results in the general expression of the potential by means of the Weyl integral (
\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
)

\Phi=-\frac{\pi^{3/2}GR_{0}^{2\gamma}\Sigma_{0}}{\Gamma(\gamma)}{{}_{\infty}^{-}I}_{R_{0}^{2}}^{\frac{3}{2}-\gamma}\left[\frac{1}{R_{0}\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+R_{0})^{2}}}\right].

(V.13)

If
\gamma<\frac{3}{2}
, this reproduces equation (
V.5
), while setting
\gamma=\frac{3}{2}+m
recovers equation (
V.10
). Lastly we establish that this satisfies the recursion relation in equation (
V.9
) following
(\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}x)^{n}({{}_{b}^{-}I}_{x}^{\eta}f)=(-1)^{n}\,{{}_{b}^{-}I}_{x}^{\eta-n}f
and
(\gamma)_{n}=\Gamma(\gamma+n)/\Gamma(\gamma)
. Thanks to mathematical induction, equation (
V.13
) is therefore the potential (with the zero-point at infinity) generated by the KMTd for
\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
.

V.2
The prolate spheroidal coordinates

Equation (
V.13
) is still too formal and the differentiation, albeit good for analytic purposes, is not suitable for numerical evaluations, especially for a higher order. Nevertheless we can utilize equation (
V.9
) to derive alternative integral quadratures (and other closed form expressions in terms of known special functions) for the potential with any
\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
.

One such possibility involves the
prolate
spheroidal coordinates (or the elliptic coordinates in the meridional plane). First let us define the distances
r_{\pm}
to two points on the
R=0
axis with
z=\mp R_{0}
as in
r_{\pm}^{2}=R^{2}+(z\pm R_{0})^{2}
. In the meridional plane, the loci of constant sums
(r_{+}+r_{-})
and differences
|r_{+}-r_{-}|
respectively trace the set of confocal ellipses and hyperbolae. Rotating around the
R=0
symmetry axis, these sets together with the meridional planes constitute the mutually orthogonal coordinate surfaces for the prolate spheroidal coordinate
(
Lynden-Bell 2003
;
An 2013
)
. We choose the coordinate variables
(\lambda,\mu,\phi)
to be the usual azimuth
\phi
and

\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{r_{+}+r_{-}}{2R_{0}},\hskip 20.00003pt\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{r_{+}-r_{-}}{2R_{0}},

(V.14)

which are consistent with equation (6.4) of
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
, although the more common choice is
(\cosh^{-1}\lambda^{1/2},\cos^{-1}\mu^{1/2},\phi)
. The triangle inequality implies that
|\mu^{1/2}|\leq 1\leq\lambda^{1/2}
and so
0\leq\mu\leq 1\leq\lambda
. Technically two points symmetric about the
z=0
plane would result in the same
(\lambda,\mu)
but
\mu^{1/2}<0
if
z<0
. Since the potential due to a razor-thin disk is reflection symmetric about the disk plane, all subsequent results involving the coordinate variables
(\lambda,\mu)
are interpreted to be valid for
z\geq 0
and the result for
z<0
is recovered through a simple reflection, e.g.,
\Phi(R,-z)=\Phi(R,z)
etc.

To find the inverse transformation
(\lambda,\mu)\to(R,z)
, note

(\lambda\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{r_{+}^{2}-r_{-}^{2}}{4R_{0}^{2}}=\frac{z}{R_{0}}=\zeta.

(V.15)

Next,
4R_{0}^{2}(\lambda,\mu)=(r_{+}^{2}+r_{-}^{2}+2r_{+}r_{-},r_{+}^{2}+r_{-}^{2}-2r_{+}r_{-})
and so

\lambda+\mu=\frac{r_{+}^{2}+r_{-}^{2}}{2R_{0}^{2}}=\frac{r^{2}+R_{0}^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\lambda-\mu=\frac{r_{+}r_{-}}{R_{0}^{2}}

where
r^{2}=R^{2}+z^{2}
. It follows that

\varrho^{2}=\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}=\lambda+\mu-\lambda\mu-1=(\lambda-1)(1-\mu).

(V.16)

Then the potentials due to the Kuzmin disk and the cored Mestel disk of the scale length
R_{0}
are expressible to be

\begin{split}\Phi_{\mathrm{K}}&=-\frac{2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}{\lambda^{1/2}+\mu^{1/2}},\\
\Phi_{\mathrm{M}}&=2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}\ln(\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}+1)(1+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}).\end{split}

(V.17)

Lastly, either from equation (
V.14
) with
\partial r_{\pm}/\partial R=R/r_{\pm}
and
\partial r_{\pm}/\partial z=(z\pm R_{0})/r_{\pm}
, or inverting the partial derivatives of equations (
V.15
) and (
V.16
) with respect to
(R,z)
, we find

\begin{pmatrix}\dfrac{\partial\lambda}{\partial(R^{2})}&\dfrac{\partial\lambda}{\partial(z^{2})}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
\dfrac{\partial\mu}{\partial(R^{2})}&\dfrac{\partial\mu}{\partial(z^{2})}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{r_{+}r_{-}}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&\lambda-1\\
-\mu&1-\mu\end{pmatrix}

(V.18)

which may be used to recover the partial derivatives of any function
f(\lambda,\mu)
with respect to
(R,z)
.

Now evaluating the definite integral

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{1/2}\mathrm{d}t}{(t+\lambda)(t+\mu)}=\frac{\pi}{\lambda^{1/2}+\mu^{1/2}}

indicates that – NB:
R_{0}^{2}(t+\lambda)(t+\mu)=(t+1)tR_{0}^{2}+r^{2}t+z^{2}
;

\frac{\pi}{R_{0}\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(z+R_{0})^{2}}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{1/2}\mathrm{d}t}{(t+1)tR_{0}^{2}+r^{2}t+z^{2}}.

(V.19)

Inserting this to equation (
V.13
) for
z>0
then yields

\frac{-\Phi(\bm{r})}{G\Sigma_{0}}=R_{0}^{2\gamma}\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}}{\Gamma(\gamma)}{{}_{\infty}^{-}I}_{R_{0}^{2}}^{\frac{3}{2}-\gamma}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{1/2}\mathrm{d}t}{(t+1)tR_{0}^{2}+r^{2}t+z^{2}}\\
=R_{0}\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\gamma)}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}t\,\frac{t^{\gamma-1}(t+1)^{\gamma-3/2}}{[(t+\lambda)(t+\mu)]^{\gamma-1/2}}.

(V.20)

Apart from differences in notation, this is identical to equation (6.2) of
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
. Here we have utilized, for
a,b,x>0
,

{{}_{\infty}^{-}I}_{x}^{\xi}(ax+b)^{-p}=\frac{\Gamma(p-\xi)}{\Gamma(p)}\frac{(ax+b)^{\xi-p}}{a^{\xi}},

(V.21)

which can be proven for
p>\xi>0
via direct calculations. Combining with
(\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}x)^{m}(ax+b)^{-p}=(-1)^{m}(p)_{m}a^{m}(ax+b)^{-p-m}
, equation (
V.21
) is also proven to hold for any
\xi<1
if
p\geq 1
. Hence equation (
V.20
) is a valid integral for the potential with
\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
. The last integral in equation (
V.20
) actually converges for
\gamma>0
. Since it also obeys the relation in equation (
V.9
), equation (
V.20
) is in fact a valid potential (up to an immaterial constant offset) for
\gamma>0
, except for the
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
case when the integral results in
\pi
and the factor
\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{1}{2})
diverges. The direct differentiations result in

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial R}&=\frac{2G\Sigma_{0}R}{R_{0}}\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\gamma+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\gamma)}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}t\,\frac{t^{\gamma}(t+1)^{\gamma-3/2}}{[(t+\lambda)(t+\mu)]^{\gamma+1/2}};\\
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z}&=\frac{2G\Sigma_{0}z}{R_{0}}\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\gamma+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\gamma)}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}t\,\frac{t^{\gamma-1}(t+1)^{\gamma-1/2}}{[(t+\lambda)(t+\mu)]^{\gamma+1/2}}.\end{split}

(V.22)

which are indeed valid if
\gamma>0
. For
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
, this reduces to

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial R}&=\frac{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}R}{R_{0}(\lambda-\mu)}\left(\frac{\lambda^{1/2}}{\lambda^{1/2}+1}-\frac{\mu^{1/2}}{1+\mu^{1/2}}\right)\\
&=\frac{2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}{r_{+}}\frac{R}{R_{0}+z+r_{+}}=\frac{2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}{R}\left(1-\frac{R_{0}+z}{r_{+}}\right);\\
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z}&=\frac{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}z}{R_{0}(\lambda-\mu)}\left(\frac{1}{\mu^{1/2}}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{1/2}}\right)=\frac{2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}{r_{+}},\end{split}

which are consistent with equation (
V.11
) assuming
z>0
(if
z<0
, then
r_{+}\to r_{-}
and
\partial\Phi/\partial z
switches the sign). If we identify with
\lim_{\gamma\downarrow 0+}[t^{\gamma-1}/\Gamma(\gamma)]=\deltaup(t)
, equations (
V.20
) and (
V.22
) also extend to include the
\gamma=0
case; namely, the infinite plate of the uniform surface density
\Sigma_{0}
, for which
\Phi=2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}\!\sqrt{\lambda\mu}=2\pi G\Sigma_{0}|z|
and
\partial\Phi/\partial z=2\pi G\Sigma_{0}z/|z|
, while
\partial\Phi/\partial R=0
: that is, at any location off the plane, the gravitational force is always vertical toward the mid-plane and independent of the height (cf.
Routh 1892
, § 22).

V.2.1
the Carlson-R and Appell-
F_{1}
functions

The integrals in equations (
V.20
) and (
V.22
) are also in the form of the Carlson-R functions (see Appendix
C
):

\begin{split}\Phi(\bm{r})&=-\frac{2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}{2\gamma-1}R_{-\frac{1}{2}}\bigl(\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2}-\gamma,\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2};\mu,1,\lambda\bigr),\\
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial R}&=\frac{4\gamma\pi G\Sigma_{0}R\,R_{-\frac{3}{2}}\bigl(\gamma+\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2}-\gamma,\gamma+\tfrac{1}{2};\mu,1,\lambda\bigr)}{(2\gamma+3)(2\gamma+1)R_{0}};\\
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z}&=\frac{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}z\,R_{-\frac{3}{2}}\bigl(\gamma+\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}-\gamma,\gamma+\tfrac{1}{2};\mu,1,\lambda\bigr)}{(2\gamma+1)R_{0}},\end{split}

(V.23)

which are valid for
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}
(but
\gamma\neq\frac{1}{2}
for
\Phi
). This is also equivalent to the bivariate hypergeometric series; that is,

\Phi(\bm{r})=-\frac{2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}{(2\gamma-1)\!\sqrt{\lambda}}F_{1}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{2};\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2}-\gamma;\gamma+\tfrac{1}{2};1-\frac{\mu}{\lambda},1-\frac{1}{\lambda}\Bigr)\\
=-\frac{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}|z|}{(2\gamma-1)\lambda^{\gamma}}F_{1}\Bigl(\gamma;\tfrac{3}{2}-\gamma,\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2};\gamma+\tfrac{1}{2};1-\mu,1-\frac{\mu}{\lambda}\Bigr),

(V.24)

where
F_{1}(a;b_{1},b_{2};c;x_{1},x_{2})
is the Appell-F
1
function,

F_{1}(a;b_{1},b_{2};c;x_{1},x_{2})=\sum_{m,n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{m+n}}{(c)_{m+n}}\frac{(b_{1})_{m}(b_{2})_{n}}{m!n!}x_{1}^{m}x_{2}^{n},

(V.25)

which is identical to the two-variable case of the Lauricella-
{\rm F_{D}}
series; i.e.,
F_{1}=F^{(2)}_{D}
with
F^{(n)}_{D}
as defined in equation (
C.5
). Similar expressions for the partial derivatives of the potential in terms of the
F_{1}
-series exist too.
Since the
F_{1}
-series of equation (
V.25
) converges for
|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|<1

(
Olver et al. 2010
, § 16.13)
and
0<\mu\leq 1\leq\lambda
for
z\neq 0
, the bivariate power-series defined by equation (
V.24
) with
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}
also converges everywhere off the mid-plane.

For
z=0
, equations (
V.23
) with
(\lambda,\mu)=(1-R^{2}/R_{0}^{2},0)
reduce to the
{{}_{2}F_{1}}
-hypergeometric functions;

\begin{split}\Phi(R,0)&=-\frac{\pi^{3/2}\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{1}{2})GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}{\Gamma(\gamma)(1+R^{2}/R_{0}^{2})^{1/2}}\,{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,\frac 32-\gamma}{1};\frac{R^{2}}{R^{2}+R_{0}^{2}}\right);\\
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial R}\Biggr\rvert_{z=0}&=\frac{\pi^{3/2}\Gamma(\gamma+\frac{1}{2})G\Sigma_{0}}{\Gamma(\gamma)(1+R^{2}/R_{0}^{2})^{3/2}}\frac{R}{R_{0}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,\frac 32-\gamma}{2};\frac{R^{2}}{R^{2}+R_{0}^{2}}\right),\end{split}

which are consistent with equation (
V.2
) thanks to the Euler–Pfaff transformation. The expression for
\partial\Phi/\partial z
in equation (
V.23
) with
z=0
is indeterminate, but there exists a transformation resulting in the proper limit:

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z}&=\frac{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}}{(2\gamma+1)\,\lambda^{\gamma}}R_{-\gamma}\bigl(\gamma+\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}-\gamma,\gamma+\tfrac{1}{2};1,\mu,\mu/\lambda\bigr)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu\to 0}}{{=}}2\pi G\Sigma_{0}(1+R^{2}/R_{0}^{2})^{-\gamma},\end{split}

which is consistent with the surface density in equation (
V.1
).

If
R=0
, then
\lambda=1
or
\mu=1
, and equation (
V.24
) becomes a single-variable hypergeometric series. In fact, setting
(\lambda,\mu)=(1,z^{2}/R_{0}^{2}<1)
and
(\lambda,\mu)=(z^{2}/R_{0}^{2}>1,1)
in equations (
V.23
) result in

\begin{split}\Phi(0,z)&=-\frac{2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}{2\gamma-1}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,\gamma-\frac 12}{\gamma+\frac 12};1-\frac{z^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right)\\
&=-\frac{2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}{2\gamma-1}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,2\gamma-1}{\gamma+\frac 12};\frac{R_{0}-|z|}{2R_{0}}\right).\end{split}

Differentiating this with respect to
z
then recovers equation (
V.3
) for
(\partial\Phi/\partial z)|_{R=0}
, whereas the axial symmetry necessitates
(\partial\Phi/\partial R)|_{R=0}=0
, which is confirmed by the corresponding result in equation (
V.23
). The result is consistent with the direct integral of the
r^{-1}
-potential from the mass element, that is,
\Phi(0,z)=-2\pi G\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}R\,R\Sigma(R)/(R^{2}+z^{2})^{1/2}
and so on.

V.3
The bivariate hypergeometric series in
(R,z)

Although equation (
V.24
) provides a series expression for the potential converging everywhere, it involves the elliptic coordinates
(\lambda,\mu)
. In order to obtain the power series of the potential in terms of the cylindrical coordinates
(R,z)
, we need to consider the Mellin transform of equation (
V.20
). Utilizing
R_{0}^{2}(t+\lambda)(t+\mu)=(t+1)tR_{0}^{2}+t(R^{2}+z^{2})+z^{2}
, we can find

\frac{\mathfrak{M}_{R^{2}\to v}\mathfrak{M}_{z^{2}\to u}(-\Phi)}{G\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{2\gamma}}=\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\gamma)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}t\,t^{\gamma-1}(t+1)^{\gamma-\frac{3}{2}}\mathcal{I};

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}R^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}z^{2}\frac{R^{2(v-1)}z^{2(u-1)}}{[(t+1)tR_{0}^{2}+t(R^{2}+z^{2})+z^{2}]^{\gamma-1/2}}\\
&=R_{0}^{2(u+v-\gamma)+1}\frac{\Gamma(v)\Gamma(u)\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{1}{2}-v-u)}{\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{1}{2})}t^{u-\gamma+\frac{1}{2}}(t+1)^{v-\gamma+\frac{1}{2}},\end{split}

and so follows

\frac{\mathfrak{M}_{R^{2}\to v}\mathfrak{M}_{z^{2}\to u}(-\Phi)}{GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}}{\Gamma(\gamma)}R_{0}^{2(u+v)}\\
\times\frac{\Gamma(v)\Gamma(u)\Gamma(u+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-v-u)\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{1}{2}-v-u)}{\Gamma(1-v)}.

The Mellin–Barnes integral representation of the potential is thus given by (
\varrho=R/R_{0}
and
\zeta=z/R_{0}
)

\Phi(R,z)=-\frac{\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}{\Gamma(\gamma)}\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\\
\times\iint\limits_{\mathcal{C}_{v}\times\mathcal{C}_{u}}\!\frac{2\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}u}{\varrho^{2v}(2\zeta)^{2u}}\frac{\Gamma(v)\Gamma(2u)\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-v-u)\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{1}{2}-v-u)}{\Gamma(1-v)},

(V.26)

This is in the form of the Meijer-G/Fox-H function of two variables
2
2

2

It appears that there is no established standard notation for the bivariate Meijer-G/Fox-H function. In this paper, to avoid unnecessary confusion, we leave the explicit double Mellin–Barnes integral for bivariate cases.

(
Agarwal 1965
;
Sharma 1965
;
Mittal & Gupta 1972
)
, but some may find it too formal. None the less, we can add up the residues contributed from the isolated poles at
(v,2u)=(-m,-n)
where
m,n
are non-negative integers, resulting in the bivariate hypergeometric series for the potential in the upper half-space (
z>0
): that is,

\frac{-\Phi(R,z)}{\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}=\sum_{m,n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-\varrho^{2})^{m}(-2\zeta)^{n}}{(m!)^{2}n!}\frac{\Gamma(m+\frac{n+1}{2})\Gamma(m+\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma)}{\Gamma(\gamma)}\\
=\frac{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\gamma)}F_{4}\bigl(\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1,\tfrac{1}{2};-\varrho^{2},\zeta^{2}\bigr)\\
-2|\zeta|\,F_{4}\bigl(1,\gamma;1,\tfrac{3}{2};-\varrho^{2},\zeta^{2}\bigr),

(V.27)

where
F_{4}(a,b;c_{1},c_{2};x_{1},x_{2})
is the Appell-F
4
functions:

F_{4}(a,b;c_{1},c_{2};x_{1},x_{2})=\sum_{m,n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{m+n}(b)_{m+n}}{(c_{1})_{m}(c_{2})_{n}}\frac{x_{1}^{m}x_{2}^{n}}{m!n!}.

Since the
F_{4}
-series converges absolutely if
|x_{1}|^{1/2}+|x_{2}|^{1/2}<1

(
Olver et al. 2010
, § 16.13)
, equation (
V.27
) converges for
R+|z|<R_{0}
(and
\gamma\neq\frac{1}{2}
). If
\gamma=0
, every term in the sum except for
(m,n)=(0,1)
is zero and so
\Phi=2\pi G\Sigma_{0}z
. If
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
, the
(m,n)=(0,0)
term is infinite but technically constant independent of
\rho
and
\zeta
. Since an additive constant in the potential is physically immaterial, skipping
(m,n)=(0,0)
in the sum with
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
actually results in a power series for the potential of the cored Mestel disk.

Equation (
V.26
) can be made to converge to its analytic continuation in the whole upper half-space with a proper choice of the integration path. In fact, unless
\gamma
is an integer, utilizing the analytic continuations
(
Olver et al. 2010
, eq. 16.16.10)
3
3

3

There is a sign error in equation 5.11.(9) of
Erdélyi et al. 1953
.
of the
F_{4}
-series, we are able to obtain alternative series expressions:

\frac{-\Phi(R,z)}{\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{1}{\gamma-1}\frac{1}{|\zeta|}F_{4}\Bigl(1,\tfrac{1}{2};1,2-\gamma;-\frac{\varrho^{2}}{\zeta^{2}},\frac{1}{\zeta^{2}}\Bigr)\\
+\frac{\Gamma(1-\gamma)\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{1}{2})}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\zeta^{2\gamma-1}}F_{4}\Bigl(\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2},\gamma;1,\gamma;-\frac{\varrho^{2}}{\zeta^{2}},\frac{1}{\zeta^{2}}\Bigr),

which converges for
|z|-R>R_{0}
, or

\frac{-\Phi(R,z)}{\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{1}{\gamma-1}\frac{1}{\varrho}F_{4}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};\tfrac{1}{2},2-\gamma;-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{\varrho^{2}},-\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}\Bigr)\\
+\frac{\Gamma(1-\gamma)\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)\varrho^{2\gamma-1}}F_{4}\Bigl(\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2},\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2};\tfrac{1}{2},\gamma;-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{\varrho^{2}},-\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}\Bigr)\\
-\frac{2|\zeta|}{\varrho^{2\gamma}}F_{4}\Bigl(\gamma,\gamma;\tfrac{3}{2},\gamma;-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{\varrho^{2}},-\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}\Bigr),

which converges for
R-|z|>R_{0}
.
The same results may also be obtained directly from equation (
V.26
) by summing up residues for poles for
\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-v-u)\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{1}{2}-v-u)
. If
\gamma
is an integer, the whole integrand of equation (
V.26
) contains second-order poles, the residue of which requires more care to calculate (the resulting series typically involves the Euler–Mascheroni constant and the logarithmic terms).

V.4
The Toomre-
n
disks: a half-integer index
\gamma

Equation (
V.10
) indicates that the potential due to the disk surface density profile of equation (
V.1
) with a non-negative integer
\gamma-\frac{3}{2}=m
can be written down as an elementary function of the coordinate variables. However it involves explicit derivatives of arbitrary order, which is impractical unless
m
is a relatively small integer. The integral representation in equation (
V.20
) with an integer
m=\gamma-\frac{3}{2}
also reduces to a closed form involving only elementary functions (e.g., through the partial fraction decomposition, the integrand is reducible to a sum of analytically integrable terms like
t^{-1/2}(t+a)^{-n}
where
a=\lambda
or
\mu
and
n
is a positive integer), but deriving general formula via this procedure is challenging. Equations (
V.23
) and (
V.24
) are still valid but the presence of special functions defeats any practical advantages afforded by purely algebraic expressions.

One route to derive explicit analytic formulae for the potential of the
Toomre 1963
Model-
n
uses the Burchnall–Chaundy expansion of the
F_{4}
-series
(
Olver et al. 2010
, eq. 16.16.7)
; namely,

F_{4}[a,b;c_{1},c_{2};x(1-y),y(1-x)]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{k}(b)_{k}(\tilde{c})_{k}}{(c_{1})_{k}(c_{2})_{k}}\\
\times\frac{(xy)^{k}}{k!}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a+k,b+k}{c_1+k};x\right){{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a+k,b+k}{c_2+k};y\right),

where
\tilde{c}=1+a+b-c_{1}-c_{2}
. If one of the upper indices of the
F_{4}
-function coincides with one of its lower indices, then

\begin{split}F_{4}&[a,b;c,b;x(1-y),y(1-x)]\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{k}(\tilde{c})_{k}(xy)^{k}}{k!(c)_{k}[(1-x)(1-y)]^{a+k}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a+k,c-b}{c+k};\frac{x}{x-1}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{(1-x)^{a}(1-y)^{a}}F_{1}\!\left(a;c-b,\tilde{c};c;\frac{x}{x-1},\frac{x}{1-x}\frac{y}{1-y}\right)\\
&=R_{-a}\!\left[b-\tilde{c},c-b,\tilde{c};(1-x)(1-y),1-y,1-x-y\right]\end{split}

where
\tilde{c}=1+a-c
. This follows the definitions of
{{}_{2}F_{1}}
- and
F_{1}
-series together with
(a)_{k}(a+k)_{m}=(a)_{k+m}
. Given
\varrho^{2}=(\lambda-1)(1-\mu)
and
\zeta^{2}=\lambda\mu
, the
F_{4}
-series in equation (
V.27
) respectively transform to

\displaystyle\begin{split}F_{4}&\bigl(\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1,\tfrac{1}{2};-\varrho^{2},\zeta^{2}\bigr)\\
&=R_{-\gamma+\frac{1}{2}}\bigl[\tfrac{1}{2},1-\gamma,\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2};1-\mu,\lambda(1-\mu),\lambda-\mu\bigr]\\
&=\frac{\!\sqrt{\lambda}}{(\lambda-\mu)^{\gamma-1/2}}R_{\gamma-\frac{3}{2}}\biggl[1-\gamma,\tfrac{1}{2},\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2};1,\lambda,\frac{\lambda(1-\mu)}{\lambda-\mu}\biggr];\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\zeta&F_{4}\bigl(1,\gamma;1,\tfrac{3}{2};-\varrho^{2},\zeta^{2}\bigr)\\
&=\!\sqrt{\lambda\mu}R_{-\gamma}\bigl[\tfrac{3}{2}-\gamma,\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};\lambda(1-\mu),\lambda-\mu,\lambda\bigr]\\
&=\frac{\!\sqrt{\mu}}{(\lambda-\mu)^{\gamma-1/2}}R_{\gamma-\frac{3}{2}}\biggl[\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2}-\gamma,\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2};1-\mu,1,\frac{\lambda(1-\mu)}{\lambda-\mu}\biggr].\end{split}

According to equation (
C.10
), the
R_{m}
-function with a non-negative integer
m=\gamma-\frac{3}{2}
is a polynomial. Following these transformations and further manipulations of multiple sums, equation (
V.27
) with a non-negative integer
m=\gamma-\frac{3}{2}
is found to be equivalent to

\frac{-\Phi(\bm{r})}{\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{2^{m+1}m!}{(2m+1)!!}\frac{\!\sqrt{\lambda}p_{m}(\lambda,\mu)-(-1)^{m}\!\sqrt{\mu}p_{m}(\mu,\lambda)}{(\lambda-\mu)^{m+1}}

(V.28)

where
(2m+1)!!=1\cdot 3\dotsm(2m-1)\cdot(2m+1)=2^{m+1}\cdot(\frac{1}{2})_{m+1}=2^{m}\cdot(\frac{3}{2})_{m}
is the double factorial and
p_{m}(x,y)
is defined to be

p_{m}(x,y)=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\sum_{j=k}^{m}\frac{(m+k)!}{k!j!(m-j)!}\frac{(\frac{1}{2})_{j-k}}{(j-k)!}\frac{(x-1)^{j}y^{k}}{(y-x)^{k}}.

In fact,
(x-y)^{m}p_{m}(x,y)
is a
m
-th polynomial of
(x,y)
: e.g.,

\begin{split}2&(x-y)p_{1}(x,y)=x^{2}+x-5xy+3y;\\
8&(x-y)^{2}p_{2}(x,y)=3x^{4}+x^{3}(2-18y)\\
&\qquad+x^{2}(3-28y+63y^{2})+10xy(3-7y)+15y^{2},\end{split}

although these become rather messy as
m
gets larger. Since
p_{0}=1
, equation (
V.28
) with
m=0
recovers
\Phi_{\mathrm{K}}
in equation (
V.17
). The potential due to
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}(1+\varrho^{2})^{-5/2}
is identified with the
m=1
case,
which simplifies to

\Phi(\bm{r})=-\frac{2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}{3}\frac{1+\lambda+\mu+3\!\sqrt{\lambda\mu}}{\bigl(\!\sqrt{\lambda}+\!\sqrt{\mu}\,\bigr)^{3}}.

This is also consistent with equation (
V.9
) applied for the potential of the Kuzmin disk following

\frac{\partial\lambda}{\partial(R_{0}^{2})}=-\frac{\lambda}{R_{0}^{2}}\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda-\mu},\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\partial\mu}{\partial(R_{0}^{2})}=-\frac{\mu}{R_{0}^{2}}\frac{1-\mu}{\lambda-\mu}.

(V.29)

V.5
