The integer index cases with elliptic integrals

According to the criteria in § 19.16(iii) of
Olver et al. 2010
,
R
-functions in equation (
V.23
) with a positive integer
\gamma
are an elliptic integral. In particular, if
\gamma=1
,

\displaystyle\frac{-\Phi(\bm{r})}{2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}=R_{F}(\mu,1,\lambda)=\frac{\mathrm{F}(\varphi,\kappa)}{\!\sqrt{\lambda-\mu}}=\frac{\mathbf{K}(\kappa)-\mathrm{F}(\phi_{\mu},\kappa)}{\!\sqrt{\lambda-\mu}};

\displaystyle\biggl(\text{where}\ \kappa^{2}=\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda-\mu},\ \cos^{2}\!\varphi=\frac{\mu}{\lambda},\ \sin^{2}\!\phi_{\mu}=\mu\biggr).

(V.30)

The last expression is consistent with the addition theorem of elliptic integrals and also follows equation (
V.27
). Here
R_{F}
is one of the Carlson symmetric elliptic integrals as in equation (
D.1a
), which is an alternative to Legendre’s integrals of the first kind,
\mathbf{K}
or
\mathrm{F}
(see App.
D.2
).

The acceleration field
\bm{\nabla}\Phi
(and the potential for any integer index other than one) additionally involves the elliptic integrals of the second kind; either the Legendre form
\mathrm{E}(\varphi,\kappa)
, or the Carlson basis function
R_{D}
. From the integral representation in equation (
V.22
),

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial R}&=\frac{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}}{3}\frac{R}{R_{0}}\frac{\lambda R_{D}(\mu,1;\lambda)-\mu R_{D}(\lambda,1;\mu)}{\lambda-\mu};\\
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z}&=\frac{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}}{3}\frac{z}{R_{0}}\frac{(\lambda-1)R_{D}(\mu,1;\lambda)+(1-\mu)R_{D}(\lambda,1;\mu)}{\lambda-\mu},\end{split}

which are consistent with the direct differentiations of equation (
V.30
) using equations (
V.18
). These are also alternatively expressible by means of
R_{F}(\mu,1,\lambda)
and
R_{D}(\mu,1;\lambda)
, or Legendre’s integrals
\mathrm{F}(\varphi,\kappa)
and
\mathrm{E}(\varphi,\kappa)
utilizing

\begin{split}\frac{\lambda-1}{3}R_{D}(\mu,1;\lambda)&=\frac{\mathrm{F}(\varphi,\kappa)-\mathrm{E}(\varphi,\kappa)}{\!\sqrt{\lambda-\mu}},\\
\frac{1-\mu}{3}R_{D}(\lambda,1;\mu)&=\frac{\lambda-1}{3}R_{D}(\mu,1;\lambda)-R_{F}(\mu,1,\lambda)+\frac{1}{\!\sqrt{\lambda\mu}}\\
&=\frac{1}{|\zeta|}-\frac{\mathrm{E}(\varphi,\kappa)}{\!\sqrt{\lambda-\mu}},\end{split}

with
(\varphi,\kappa)
as defined in equation (
V.30
). In the
z=0
limit,
\lim_{z\downarrow 0^{+}}(\partial\Phi/\partial z)=2\pi G\Sigma_{0}/\lambda
and
(\lambda,\mu)=(1+\varrho^{2},0)
, which is consistent with the surface density of equation (
V.1
) with
\gamma=1
. The rotation curve follows
\partial\Phi/\partial R
in the same limit,

\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{\varrho^{2}}{3}R_{D}(0,1;1+\varrho^{2})=\frac{\mathbf{K}(\kappa_{0})-\mathbf{E}(\kappa_{0})}{\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}},

where
\kappa_{0}^{2}=\varrho^{2}/(1+\varrho^{2})=R^{2}/(R^{2}+R_{0}^{2})
, which compares to the behavior of the mid-plane potential;
-\Phi(R,0)/(2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0})=R_{F}(0,1,1+\varrho^{2})=(1+\varrho^{2})^{-1/2}\mathbf{K}(\kappa_{0})
.

The potentials and accelerations for
\Sigma\propto(1+\varrho^{2})^{-m}
with any positive integer
m
are expressible using only algebraic functions of
(\mu,\lambda)
with the elliptic integral basis set consisting of either
\set{R_{F}(\mu,1,\lambda),R_{D}(\mu,1;\lambda)}
or
\set{\mathrm{F}(\varphi,\kappa),\mathrm{E}(\varphi,\kappa)}
thanks to the recurrence relation in equation (
V.9
) together with the partial derivatives of equation (
V.29
). In practice, not only deriving such an expression is rather tedious but also the resulting expressions are not necessarily any more useful than unresolved integral representations such as equations (
V.20
) and (
V.22
). Here we only provide the explicit expression for
\gamma=2
(cf.
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
, table 3)

\begin{split}\frac{-\Phi(\bm{r})}{\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}&=R_{F}-\frac{\lambda(\lambda-1)R_{D}+\mu(1-\mu)R_{D}(\lambda,1;\mu)}{3(\lambda-\mu)}\\
&=\frac{1}{\lambda-\mu}\left[\lambda R_{F}-\frac{(\lambda-1)(\lambda+\mu)}{3}R_{D}-\!\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\lambda}}\right]\\
&=\frac{(\lambda+\mu)\mathrm{E}(\varphi,\kappa)-\mu\mathrm{F}(\varphi,\kappa)}{(\lambda-\mu)^{3/2}}-\frac{1}{\lambda-\mu}\!\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\lambda}},\end{split}

where
R_{F}=R_{F}(\mu,1,\lambda)
and
R_{D}=R_{D}(\mu,1;\lambda)
. The first line follows either applying equation (
V.9
) on equation (
V.30
) or resolving equation (
V.20
) via the partial fraction decomposition. On the mid-plane, this results in
-\Phi(R,0)/(\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0})=R_{F}(0,1,1+\varrho^{2})-(\varrho^{2}/3)R_{D}(0,1;1+\varrho^{2})=(1+\varrho^{2})^{-1/2}\mathbf{E}(\kappa_{0})
with the corresponding circular speed given by

\begin{split}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}&=\frac{\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}\varrho^{2}}{1+\varrho^{2}}\left[R_{F}(0,1,1+\varrho^{2})+\frac{1-\varrho^{2}}{3}R_{D}(0,1;1+\varrho^{2})\right]\\
&=\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}\frac{\mathbf{K}(\kappa_{0})+(\varrho^{2}-1)\mathbf{E}(\kappa_{0})}{(1+\varrho^{2})^{3/2}}.\end{split}

VI
The Qian–Kalnajs–Mestel disks

The
1
weight of the KMTd is in the form of
G^{0,1}_{1,1}
with one index fixed to be zero (eq.
V.4
). Let us generalize this to
G^{0,1}_{1,1}
with two free indices. In particular, consider the disk with the surface density of the form

\begin{split}\frac{\Sigma(R)}{\Sigma_{0}}&={{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,\frac 32}{b};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right)\\
&=\frac{2\Gamma(b)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(a)}G^{1,2}_{2,2}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-\frac 12,1-a}{0,1-b}\right),\end{split}

(VI.1)

which is one of the particular cases (with the redefined scale constants) of equation (
IV.15
) for which both
\Sigma(R)
and
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
are expressible through a single
{{}_{2}F_{1}}
-series. This is equivalent to the
m=0
case of equation (4.7) of
Qian 1992
. As is noted by
Qian 1992
, this family encompasses the so-called
Kalnajs 1976
–
Mestel 1963
disk family
(
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
)
, which corresponds to
(a,b)=(m/2,1+m/2)
with
m
being a positive integer. Since
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}/(1+\varrho^{2})^{a}
with
\varrho=R/R_{0}
if
b=\frac{3}{2}
, the family includes all the KMTd with the cored Mestel disk resulting from
(a,b)=(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2})
and the
a=b
case reducing to the Kuzmin disk. Also included in this family is the projected isochrone found in equation (6.21) of
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
, which results from
(a,b)=(2,\frac{5}{2})
. Henceforth we refer to this family as the QKMd (after
Qian 1992
–
Kalnajs 1976
–
Mestel 1963
disks).

Equation (
VI.1
) indicates that
\Sigma(R)/\Sigma_{0}\simeq 1-3a\varrho^{2}/(2b)
for
R\ll R_{0}
, and so the surface density for
b>0
is finite at the center with
\Sigma(0)=\Sigma_{0}
(the
a=0
case becoming the uniform plate
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}
). On the other hand, the asymptotic behavior of
\Sigma(R)
as
R\to\infty
is found to be (assuming
b\neq a
and
b\neq\frac{3}{2}
)

\frac{\Sigma}{\Sigma_{0}}\simeq\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{2\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-a)\Gamma(b)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(b-a)}\frac{1}{\varrho^{2a}}&(a<\tfrac{3}{2})\\
\displaystyle\frac{2\Gamma(b)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(b-\frac{3}{2})}\frac{\ln(4\varrho^{2})-\digamma(b-\frac{3}{2})-\gammaup-2}{\varrho^{3}}&(a=\tfrac{3}{2})\\
\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(a-\frac{3}{2})\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b-\frac{3}{2})}\frac{1}{\varrho^{3}}&(a>\tfrac{3}{2})\end{cases},

where
\gammaup\approx 0.5772
is the Euler–Mascheroni constant and
\digamma(x)=\mathrm{d}\ln|\Gamma(x)|/\mathrm{d}x
is the digamma function. Note that
\Sigma(R)\to 0
as
R\to\infty
if
a>0
. The enclosed mass,
M(R)=2\pi\int_{0}^{R}\mathrm{d}\tilde{R}\tilde{R}\Sigma(\tilde{R})
, is given by

M(R)=\frac{2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,R_{0}^{2}\Sigma_{0}\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)}G^{1,3}_{3,3}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,\frac 12,2-a}{1,0,2-b}\right),

which behaves like
M(R)\simeq\pi\Sigma_{0}R^{2}
as
R\to 0
and like

\frac{M(R)}{R_{0}^{2}\Sigma_{0}}\simeq\begin{cases}\dfrac{2\pi(b-1)}{a-1}&(a>1)\\
2\pi(b-1)\left[\ln\dfrac{\varrho^{2}}{4}-\digamma(b-1)-\gammaup\right]&(a=1)\\
\dfrac{2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(b)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-a)}{(1-a)\Gamma(b-a)}\varrho^{2(1-a)}&(a<1)\end{cases}

as
R\to\infty
. If
a,b>1
, the total mass of the disk is a well-defined positive finite value
M_{\mathrm{t}}=2\pi R_{0}^{2}\Sigma_{0}(b-1)/(a-1)
. The non-positive mass for
b\leq 1
is the consequence of the negative surface density at large radii, which is unphysical. In fact,
b\geq\min(a,\frac{3}{2})
is also necessary for
\Sigma
to be non-negative. For the disk model with the finite total mass, the mass profile can be expressed slightly simpler as

\begin{split}1-\frac{M(R)}{M_{\mathrm{t}}}&=\frac{\Gamma(b-1)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(a-1)}G^{1,2}_{2,2}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,2-a}{0,2-b}\right)\\
&={{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,a-1}{b-1};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right).\end{split}

The gravitational force due to the disk is found for models with
a>-\frac{1}{2}
and the resulting mid-plane rotation curve is

\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)=\frac{2\pi\Gamma(a+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b+\frac{1}{2})}\frac{G\Sigma_{0}R^{2}}{R_{0}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,a+\frac 12}{b+\frac 12};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right),

(VI.2)

whose
R\to\infty
behavior, assuming
b\neq 1
, is like

\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}\simeq\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(a+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(1-a)\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b-a)}\frac{4\!\sqrt{\pi}}{\varrho^{2a-1}}&(a<1)\\
\displaystyle 2\pi(b-1)\frac{\ln(4\varrho^{2})-\digamma(b-1)-\gammaup-2}{\varrho}&(a=1)\\
\displaystyle\frac{b-1}{a-1}\frac{2\pi}{\varrho}=\frac{1}{R_{0}\Sigma_{0}}\frac{M_{\mathrm{t}}}{R}&(a>1)\end{cases}.

If
b=1
, then
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\propto\varrho^{2}(1+\varrho^{2})^{-(a+1/2)}
and so
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\sim\varrho^{1-2a}
as
R\to\infty
for any
a>0
(but only physical if
a\leq b=1
). For finite mass (
a>1
) disks, this is consistent with the Keplerian behavior,
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\simeq GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R
as
R\to\infty
, whereas the asymptotic behavior
\Sigma\sim R^{-2a}
for
a<1
results in a slower fall-off of the circular speed
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}\sim R^{a-1/2}
with the
a=\frac{1}{2}
case leading to the asymptotically flat rotation curve, as suggested by
\Sigma\sim R^{-1}
as
R\to\infty
.

On the other hand, the vertical force on the symmetry axis is given by (
\zeta=z/R_{0}
)

\displaystyle\frac{\varg(z)}{G\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)}G^{2,3}_{3,3}\!\left(\frac{z^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{0,-\frac 12,1-a}{0,\frac 12,1-b}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(k+1)\Gamma(a+k/2)}{\Gamma(b+k/2)}(-\zeta)^{k}

(VI.3)

\displaystyle=4\pi\left[F^{+}(\zeta^{2})-\frac{\Gamma(b)\Gamma(a+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b+\frac{1}{2})}\zeta{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{2,a+\frac 12}{b+\frac 12};\zeta^{2}\right)\right]

where the even-part function is

\begin{split}F^{+}(\zeta^{2})&=\frac{1}{2}\,{{}_{3}F_{2}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,\frac 32,a}{\frac 12,b};\zeta^{2}\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+\tfrac{1}{2})\frac{(a)_{k}}{(b)_{k}}\zeta^{2k}\\
&={{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{2,a}{b};\zeta^{2}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\,{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,a}{b};\zeta^{2}\right)\\
&=\frac{b-1}{1-\zeta^{2}}-\frac{b-\frac{3}{2}-(a-\frac{1}{2})\zeta^{2}}{1-\zeta^{2}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,a}{b};\zeta^{2}\right).\end{split}

Also note that
\varg(0)=2\pi G\Sigma_{0}
, which relates to the finite central surface density, whereas the asymptotic behavior as
z\to\infty
is

\frac{\varg(z)}{G\Sigma_{0}}\simeq\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{4\pi^{2}(1-2a)\Gamma(b)}{\sin(2a\pi)\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b-a)}\frac{1}{\zeta^{2a}}&(a<1)\\
\displaystyle 2\pi(b-1)\frac{\ln\zeta^{2}-\digamma(b-1)-\gammaup-2}{\zeta^{2}}&(a=1)\\
\displaystyle\frac{b-1}{a-1}\frac{2\pi}{\zeta^{2}}=\frac{M_{\mathrm{t}}}{\Sigma_{0}}\frac{1}{z^{2}}&(a>1)\end{cases},

which are valid for
b\neq 1
– NB:
\lim_{a\to 1/2}(1-2a)\pi/\sin(2a\pi)=\lim_{a\to 1/2}\Gamma(2a)\Gamma(2-2a)=1
. If
b=1
on the other hand,

\begin{split}\frac{\varg(z)}{G\Sigma_{0}}&=\frac{2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(a+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(a+2)}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,\frac 32}{a+2};1-\zeta^{2}\right)\\
&\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle z\to\infty}}{{\simeq}}\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{2\pi(1-2a)}{\cos(a\pi)}\frac{1}{\zeta^{2a}}&(a<\frac{3}{2})\\
\displaystyle\frac{4[\ln(4\zeta^{2})-3]}{\zeta^{3}}&(a=\frac{3}{2})\\
\displaystyle\frac{2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(a-\frac{3}{2})}{\Gamma(a)}\frac{1}{\zeta^{3}}&(a>\frac{3}{2})\end{cases},\end{split}

although only those models with
-\frac{1}{2}<a\leq b=1
result in
the non-negative surface density and the finite gravity.

VI.1
The potentials due to the Qian–Kalnajs–Mestel disks

The
1
weight for the QKMd follows equation (
IV.17
), which is an elementary function (
b>a>0
):

\begin{split}\Lambda(h)&=\frac{4\pi\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)}\Sigma_{0}R_{0}\,G^{0,1}_{1,1}\!\left(\frac{h^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32-a}{\frac 32-b}\right)\\
&=\frac{4\pi\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{2a}\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b-a)}\frac{(h^{2}-R_{0}^{2})^{b-a-1}}{h^{2b-3}}\Theta(h-R_{0}).\end{split}

(VI.4)

Once equation (
VI.4
) is chosen as the weight, equations (
III.2
) result in the
{}_{2}F_{1}
-hypergeometric functions in equations (
VI.1
) and (
VI.2
). Hence the potential generated by the QKMd can be written down as a quadrature like

\frac{\Phi(R,z)}{G\Sigma_{0}}=-\frac{4\pi R_{0}^{2a}\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b-a)}\int_{R_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{(h^{2}-R_{0}^{2})^{b-a-1}\mathrm{d}h}{h^{2b-3}\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+h)^{2}}},

(VI.5)

which converges if
b>a>\frac{1}{2}
. The change of the integration variable,
h\to x
, given by
2(h+|z|)=(x+|z|)-R^{2}/(x+|z|)
leads this to a manifest integral form of the Carlson-R function;

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi(R,z)}{G\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2^{2a+1}\pi R_{0}^{2a}\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b-a)}\!\!\int_{R_{0}+r_{+}}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}x\frac{(x+|z|)^{2a-2}}{(x^{2}-r^{2})^{2b-3}}\mathcal{Q}^{b-a-1};

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}

\displaystyle=\bigl[(x-R_{0})^{2}-r_{+}^{2}\bigr]\bigl[(x+R_{0})^{2}-r_{-}^{2}\bigr],

(VI.6)

where
r^{2}=R^{2}+z^{2}
and
r_{\pm}^{2}=R^{2}+(|z|\pm R_{0})^{2}
. Provided that both
2a
and
2b
are integers, this is either resolved into an expression involving only elementary functions (if
b-a
is also an integer) or an elliptic integral (if
b-a-1/2
is an integer). The reduction to elliptic integrals however is simpler if the elliptic coordinates
(\lambda,\mu)
defined in Sect.
V.2
– i.e.
(R/R_{0})^{2}=(\lambda-1)(1-\mu)
and
(z/R_{0})^{2}=\lambda\mu
– and an alternative change of the integration variable,
h\to y
, given by
h/R_{0}=[\!\sqrt{\lambda(y+\lambda)}-\!\sqrt{\mu(y+\mu)}]/[\!\sqrt{\lambda(y+\mu)}-\!\sqrt{\mu(y+\lambda)}]
are used. This then reduces equation (
VI.5
) to

\displaystyle\Phi(\bm{r})=-\frac{2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b-a)}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathcal{F}^{2a-2}\mathcal{G}^{3-2b}\mathrm{d}y}{\!\sqrt{(y+\mu)(y+1)(y+\lambda)}};

(VI.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}&=\frac{\!\sqrt{\lambda(y+\mu)}-\!\sqrt{\mu(y+\lambda)}}{\lambda-\mu}=\frac{y}{\!\sqrt{\lambda(y+\mu)}+\!\sqrt{\mu(y+\lambda)}},\\
\mathcal{G}&=\frac{\!\sqrt{\lambda(y+\lambda)}-\!\sqrt{\mu(y+\mu)}}{\lambda-\mu}=\frac{y+\lambda+\mu}{\!\sqrt{\lambda(y+\lambda)}+\!\sqrt{\mu(y+\mu)}}.\end{split}

In general, this is not in the form of a single Carlson-R, although the
(a,b)=(1,\frac{3}{2})
case recovers equation (
V.30
). Instead, if
2a
and
2b
are both integers, this is expressible as a finite sum of Carlson-R’s (which reduces to the Carlson symmetric basis or an elementary function) expanding
\mathcal{F}^{2a-2}\mathcal{G}^{3-2b}
.

Equation (
VI.5
) is also recognized as the Weyl integral. In fact, the generalization given by (
r^{2}=R^{2}+z^{2}
)

\frac{\Phi(R,z)}{G\Sigma_{0}}=-2\pi R_{0}^{2a}\frac{\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)}\,{{}_{\infty}^{-}I_{R_{0}^{2}}^{b-a}}\left[\frac{R_{0}^{2(1-b)}}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+R_{0})^{2}}}\right]\\
=-2R_{0}^{2a}\frac{\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}t\,t^{\frac{1}{2}}\,{{}_{\infty}^{-}I_{R_{0}^{2}}^{b-a}}\left[\frac{R_{0}^{3-2b}}{(t+1)tR_{0}^{2}+r^{2}t+z^{2}}\right]

(VI.8)

provides the general formula for the potential due to the QKMd with
a,b>\frac{1}{2}
. This is proven by establishing the recursion relation and its generalization via the fractional integral applicable to the surface density in equation (
VI.1
), viz.

\left[-\frac{\partial}{\partial(R_{0}^{2})}\right]^{m}\,\frac{1}{R_{0}^{2a}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,\frac 32}{b};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right)\\
=\frac{(a)_{m}}{R_{0}^{2(a+m)}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a+m,\frac 32}{b};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right);

and

{{}_{\infty}^{-}I_{R_{0}^{2}}^{\xi}}\Biggl[\frac{1}{R_{0}^{2a}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,\frac 32}{b};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right)\Biggr]\\
=\frac{\Gamma(a-\xi)}{\Gamma(a)}\frac{1}{R_{0}^{2(a-\xi)}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a-\xi,\frac 32}{b};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right).

Since the model with
a=b
is the Kuzmin disk, we find

{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,\frac 32}{b};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right)=R_{0}^{2a}\frac{\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)}\,{{}_{\infty}^{-}I_{R_{0}^{2}}^{b-a}}\frac{1}{R_{0}^{2b}}\left(1+\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}},

and then equation (
VI.8
) follows the linearity of the Poisson equation. Utilizing the formula

{{}_{\infty}^{-}I_{x}^{\xi}}\left[\frac{x^{\eta}}{(ax+b)^{p}}\right]=\frac{\Gamma(p-\xi-\eta)\,x^{\eta+\xi}}{\Gamma(p-\eta)\,(ax+b)^{p}}\,{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,\xi}{p-\eta};\frac{b}{ax+b}\right),

equation (
VI.8
) also reduces to a quadrature given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}=-&\frac{2\Gamma(b)\Gamma(a-\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b-\frac{1}{2})}\!\\
&\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}t\,t^{1/2}}{(t+\lambda)(t+\mu)}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,b-a}{b-\frac 12};\mathcal{X}\right);\end{split}

(VI.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}=1-\frac{t(t+1)}{(t+\mu)(t+\lambda)}=\frac{1}{\lambda-\mu}\left[\frac{(\lambda-1)\lambda}{t+\lambda}+\frac{(1-\mu)\mu}{t+\mu}\right],

where
(\lambda,\mu)
are the elliptic coordinates defined in Sect
V.2
, and so
(t+1)tR_{0}^{2}+r^{2}t+z^{2}=R_{0}^{2}(t+\lambda)(t+\mu)
. If
(a,b)=(\gamma,\frac{3}{2})
here, equation (
VI.9
) reproduces equation (
V.20
).

Equation (
VI.5
) with
b=\frac{1}{2}
does not converge but equation (
VI.9
) reduces to a definite limit for
b=\frac{1}{2}
via
\lim_{c\to 0}{{}_{2}F_{1}}(a,b;c;x)/\Gamma(c)=abx{{}_{2}F_{1}}(a+1,b+1;2;x)
:

\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(a+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(a)}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}t\,t^{a-1}(t+1)^{a-3/2}}{[(t+\mu)(t+\lambda)]^{a-1/2}}\,\mathcal{X},

(VI.10)

which converges for
a>0
. This can be extended to the
a=0
case by adopting
\lim_{a\downarrow 0^{+}}t^{a-1}/\Gamma(a)=\deltaup(t)
and then
\Phi=2\pi G\Sigma_{0}R_{0}(\lambda\mu)^{1/2}=2\pi G\Sigma_{0}|z|
. It is verifiable that equation (
VI.10
) is consistent with the “potential” due to the surface density in equation (
VI.1
) with
b=\frac{1}{2}
which reduces to

\Sigma(R)=\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{2a}\frac{(1-2a)R^{2}+R_{0}^{2}}{(R^{2}+R_{0}^{2})^{a+1}},

although this is non-negative everywhere only if
a\leq\frac{1}{2}
(and
\Sigma\to 0
as
R\to\infty
for
a>0
). The
z=0
potential resulting from equation (
VI.10
) with
\mu=0
and
\lambda=1+R^{2}/R_{0}^{2}
,

\Phi(R,0)=\frac{\pi^{3/2}\Gamma(a+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(a)}\frac{G\Sigma_{0}R^{2}}{R_{0}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,a+\frac 12}{2};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right),

(VI.11)

is consistent with the rotation curve in equation (
VI.2
)

\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)=\frac{2\pi^{3/2}\Gamma(a+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(a)}\frac{G\Sigma_{0}R^{2}}{R_{0}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,a+\frac 12}{1};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right),

but the zero-point for the potential in equation (
VI.11
) is at the origin and the potential for
a<\frac{1}{2}
diverges like
\sim R^{1-2a}
as
R\to\infty
. By contrast,
\lim_{R\to\infty}\Phi(R,0)=2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}
for
a=\frac{1}{2}
, whereas
\lim_{R\to\infty}\Phi=0
if
a>\frac{1}{2}
, and
\Phi(R,0)
is not monotonic for those cases (and so
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}<0
for large radii and also
\Sigma<0
). On the other hand, equation (
VI.10
) with
(\lambda,\mu)=(z^{2}/R_{0}^{2}>1,1)
or
(\lambda,\mu)=(1,z^{2}/R_{0}^{2}<1)
results in the potential along the symmetry axis expressible using a
{{}_{2}F_{1}}
-function;

\Phi(0,z)=\frac{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}|z|}{2a+1}\,{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,1}{a+\frac 32};1-\frac{z^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right).

Although it is not immediately obvious, this is consistent with the earlier result in equation (
VI.3
) with
b=\frac{1}{2}
.

Equation (
VI.10
) is further reducible to closed-form expressions utilizing the Carlson-R functions,

\begin{split}\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}&=2\pi\,\biggl[R_{-\frac{1}{2}}\bigl(a-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2}-a,a-\tfrac{1}{2};\mu,1,\lambda\bigr)\\
&\qquad-\frac{2a}{2a+1}R_{-\frac{1}{2}}\bigl(a+\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}-a,a+\tfrac{1}{2};\mu,1,\lambda\bigr)\biggr]\\
\phantom{\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}}&=\frac{2\pi}{2a+1}\Biggl[\frac{\lambda(\lambda-1)}{\lambda-\mu}\,R_{-\frac{3}{2}}\bigl(a-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2}-a,a+\tfrac{1}{2};\mu,1,\lambda\bigr)\\
&\qquad+\frac{\mu(1-\mu)}{\lambda-\mu}\,R_{-\frac{3}{2}}\bigl(a+\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2}-a,a-\tfrac{1}{2};\mu,1,\lambda\bigr)\Biggr].\end{split}

The result for
a=0
is consistent with the potential due to an infinite plate (
\Phi\propto|z|
) whereas the result with
a=\frac{1}{2}(=b)
is reducible to
\Phi=2\pi G\Sigma_{0}R_{0}[1-(\!\sqrt{\lambda}+\!\sqrt{\mu})^{-1}]
, which is the zero-point-shifted potential due to the Kuzmin disk.

If
a-b
is a non-negative integer, equation (
VI.8
) is purely differential and so reducible to a completely analytic expression. In particular, if
a=b+1
, we have

\begin{split}\Sigma(R)&=\frac{\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{3}}{(R^{2}+R_{0}^{2})^{3/2}}\left(1-\frac{3}{2b}\frac{R^{2}}{R^{2}+R_{0}^{2}}\right);\\
\Phi(\bm{r})&=-\frac{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{2}}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+R_{0})^{2}}}\left(1-\frac{1}{b}+\frac{R_{0}}{2b}\frac{|z|+R_{0}}{R^{2}+(|z|+R_{0})^{2}}\right),\\
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)&=\frac{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{2}R^{2}}{(R_{0}+R^{2})^{3/2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{2b}-\frac{3}{2b}\frac{R^{2}}{R^{2}+R_{0}^{2}}\right);\\
\varg(z)&=\frac{2\pi G\Sigma_{0}R_{0}^{2}}{(z+R_{0})^{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{b}\frac{z}{z+R_{0}}\right)\quad(z>0).\end{split}

Here the surface density is non-negative everywhere if
b\geq\frac{3}{2}
.

VI.2
The Kalnajs–Mestel disks

Equation (
VI.5
) with a positive integer value of
b-a=n+1
, is reducible to a closed form consisting entirely of elementary functions if
2a-1
is also a positive integer. In particular, if
n=0
and
a\in\set{1,\frac{3}{2},2,\cdots}
, equation (
VI.6
) is resolved via the standard techniques such as partial fraction decompositions: e.g., if
(a,b)=(1,2)
,

\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}=-\frac{8\pi}{\sigma}\tanh^{-1}\biggl(\frac{\sigma}{1+\tau_{+}}\biggr),

(VI.12)

whereas, if
(a,b)=(\frac{3}{2},\frac{5}{2})
,

\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}=-\frac{6\pi}{\sigma^{2}}\left[\tau_{+}-1-\frac{2|\zeta|}{\sigma}\tanh^{-1}\biggl(\frac{\sigma}{1+\tau_{+}}\biggr)\right].

(VI.13)

Here
\sigma^{2}=(r/R_{0})^{2}=\varrho^{2}+\zeta^{2}
and
\tau_{\pm}^{2}=(r_{\pm}/R_{0})^{2}=\varrho^{2}+(|\zeta|\pm 1)^{2}
with
(\varrho,\zeta)=(R/R_{0},z/R_{0})
. Also note
2\lambda^{1/2}=\tau_{+}+\tau_{-}
and
2\mu^{1/2}=\tau_{+}-\tau_{-}
. If
a=\frac{1}{2}
, equation (
VI.5
) does not converge, but the zero-point-shifted potential (cf. eq.
II.5
) can still be obtained by the alternative kernel
[R^{2}+(|z|+t)^{2}]^{1/2}-t^{-1}
. In particular, for
(a,b)=(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2})
, the same variable change as equation (
VI.6
) results in

\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{2\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}=\int_{R_{0}}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}h\left(\frac{1}{h}-\frac{1}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+h)^{2}}}\right)\\
=\int_{R_{0}+r_{+}}^{\infty}\!\frac{2(|z|x+r^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x}{(x+|z|)(x^{2}-r^{2})}=\sinh^{-1}\biggl(\frac{1+|\zeta|}{\varrho}\biggr)+\ln\frac{\varrho}{2}

which recovers the potential of a cored Mestel disk (eq.
II.9
) with the zero-point at the origin,
\Phi(0,0)=0
.

The corresponding
\Sigma(R)
and
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
are expressed as

\begin{split}\frac{\Sigma(R)}{\Sigma_{0}}&={{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,\frac 32}{a+1};-\varrho^{2}\right)=\frac{a}{\varrho^{2a}}B_{\kappa_{0}^{2}}\!\left(a,\tfrac{3}{2}-a\right);\\
\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}&=\frac{2\pi a\,\varrho^{2}}{a+\frac{1}{2}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,a+\frac 12}{a+\frac 32};-\varrho^{2}\right)=\frac{2\pi a}{\varrho^{2a-1}}B_{\kappa_{0}^{2}}\!\left(a+\tfrac{1}{2},1-a\right),\end{split}

where
\kappa_{0}^{2}=\varrho^{2}/(1+\varrho^{2})
and

B_{z}(a,b)={\rm B}(z;a,b)=\int_{0}^{z}\!u^{a-1}(1-u)^{b-1}\mathrm{d}u

is the incomplete beta function,
which is reducible to an elementary function using the formulae:

\displaystyle B_{\kappa_{0}^{2}}\left(k+1,\tfrac{1}{2}-k\right)=\frac{2(-1)^{k}k!}{(\frac{1}{2})_{k}}\left[1-\frac{1}{\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{\frac{1}{2}}{j}\,\varrho^{2j}\right];

\displaystyle\begin{split}B_{\kappa_{0}^{2}}&\left(k+\tfrac{3}{2},-k\right)=\frac{2(-1)^{k}(\frac{3}{2})_{k}}{k!}\\
&\times\left[\sinh^{-1}\varrho-\frac{\varrho}{\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}}\,\Biggl(1-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{(j-1)!}{2}\frac{(-\varrho^{2})^{j}}{(\frac{3}{2})_{j}}\Biggr)\right],\end{split}

for any non-negative integer
k
. That is to say, if
a
is a half integer,
\Sigma(R)
involves
\sinh^{-1}\varrho
– except
a=\frac{1}{2}
for which
\Sigma\propto(1+\varrho^{2})^{-1/2}
– but
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
is entirely algebraic, whereas
\Sigma(R)
is algebraic and
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
involves
\sinh^{-1}\varrho
for a (positive) integer value of
a
. The associated vertical acceleration on the symmetry axis is found to be (
z>0
)

\frac{\varg(z)}{G\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{4\pi a}{\zeta^{2a}}\,{\rm B}\biggl(\frac{\zeta}{1+\zeta};2a,2-2a\biggr),

which can be expressed as an elementary function,

\frac{\varg(z)}{4\pi aG\Sigma_{0}}=m\left[\frac{(-1)^{m+1}}{\zeta^{m+1}}\ln(1+\zeta)+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{m+1-k}\frac{1}{\zeta^{k}}\right]\\
+\frac{1}{1+\zeta},

if
m=2a-1
is a non-negative integer.

VI.2.1
The Kalnajs isochrone disk vs. the projected isochrone

The mid-plane potential for the
(a,b)=(\frac{3}{2},\frac{5}{2})
case

-\Phi(R,0)=6\pi GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}\frac{\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}-1}{\varrho^{2}}=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{R_{0}+(R^{2}+R_{0}^{2})^{1/2}}

has the same functional form as the spherical isochrone potential
Hénon 1959
of the same mass
M_{\mathrm{t}}=6\pi R_{0}^{2}\Sigma_{0}
and scale length
h=R_{0}
, i.e.
\Phi(r)=-GM_{\mathrm{t}}/[h+\!\sqrt{h^{2}+r^{2}}]
. In other words, the mid-plane rotation curve due to the disk with

\begin{split}\Sigma(R)&=\frac{M_{\mathrm{t}}R_{0}}{2\pi R^{3}}\left[\sinh^{-1}\biggl(\frac{R}{R_{0}}\biggr)-\frac{R}{(R_{0}^{2}+R^{2})^{1/2}}\right];\\
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)&=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}R^{2}}{(R_{0}^{2}+R^{2})^{1/2}\,\bigl[R_{0}+(R_{0}^{2}+R^{2})^{1/2}\bigr]^{2}}\end{split}

is identical to the circular speed curve of the spherical isochrone potential
(
Kalnajs 1976
)
. The vertical force along the symmetry axis due to this disk is given by

\varg(z)=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{z^{2}}\left[\frac{z+2R_{0}}{z+R_{0}}-\frac{2R_{0}}{z}\ln\biggl(1+\frac{z}{R_{0}}\biggr)\right]

The expression for the full potential is provided in equation (
VI.13
) and also found in
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
.

However the surface density profile in equation (33) of
Hénon 1959
corresponds to the projected density of the spherical profile. Curiously its functional form (cf.
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
, eq. 6.21)

\frac{\Sigma(R)}{\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{3}{2\varrho^{3}}\left(\tan^{-1}\varrho-\frac{\varrho}{1+\varrho^{2}}\right)={{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,2}{\frac 52};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right)

is in the form of equation (
VI.1
) with
(a,b)=(2,\frac{5}{2})
. Yet, the rotation curve due to the disk of the same density profile is

\begin{split}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)&=\frac{9\pi^{2}}{16}GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}\,\varrho^{2}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,\frac 52}{3};-\varrho^{2}\right)\\
&=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{R_{0}}\left[R_{F}(0,1,1+\varrho^{2})-\frac{2+\varrho^{2}}{3}R_{D}(0,1;1+\varrho^{2})\right]\\
&=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{R_{0}}\frac{(2+\varrho^{2})\mathbf{E}(\kappa_{0})-2\mathbf{K}(\kappa_{0})}{\varrho^{2}\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}};\quad\kappa_{0}^{2}=\frac{\varrho^{2}}{1+\varrho^{2}},\end{split}

where
M_{\mathrm{t}}=3\pi R_{0}^{2}\Sigma_{0}
is the disk mass. The maximum of this curve is higher than the circular velocity due to the spherical isochrone of the same mass, which is expected. The expression of the potential due to this disk is provided in equation (6.23) of
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
, which involves elliptic integrals
4
4

4

Equation (
VI.5
) is an elliptic integral if
b-a
is a half-integer and
2a
is an integer. If
a
is an integer and
b
is a half-integer,
\Sigma(R)
is elementary but
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
requires elliptic integrals, whereas, if
a
is a half-integer and
b
is an integer,
\Sigma(R)
is an elliptic integral but
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
is elementary.
.

VI.3
The projected column density of the spherical profile

As a brief digression, let us ask how a projected column density of the spherical profile is related to the disk surface density if the rotation curves due to both models are the same. If
\rho(r)
is the spherical profile with the radius
r
, then its projected column density is given by

\Sigma^{\mathrm{p}}(R)=2\!\int_{R}^{\infty}\!\frac{r\rho(r)\,\mathrm{d}r}{\!\sqrt{r^{2}-R^{2}}}.

Since the rotation curve of the spherical profile is found to be
\varv_{\rm rot}^{2}(r)=GM(r)/r=4\pi Gr^{-1}\!\int_{0}^{r}\mathrm{d}\tilde{r}\,\tilde{r}^{2}\rho(\tilde{r})
, the projected profile can be directly obtained from the rotation curve via:

\Sigma^{\mathrm{p}}(R)=\frac{1}{2\pi G}\!\int_{R}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r\!\sqrt{r^{2}-R^{2}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}(r\varv_{\rm rot}^{2})}{\mathrm{d}r}.

From the Mellin transform
R^{2}\to t
of this, we find

\begin{split}\mathfrak{M}[\Sigma^{\mathrm{p}}](t)&=\frac{(1-t)\Gamma(t)}{\Gamma(t+\frac{1}{2})}\frac{\mathfrak{M}[\varv_{\rm rot}^{2}]\bigl(t-\tfrac{1}{2}\bigr)}{2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,G};\\
\frac{\mathfrak{M}[\varv_{\rm rot}^{2}](t)}{2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,G}&=\frac{\Gamma(t+1)\,\mathfrak{M}[\Sigma^{\mathrm{p}}](t+\tfrac{1}{2})}{(\frac{1}{2}-t)\Gamma(t+\frac{1}{2})}.\end{split}

(VI.14)

Given the Meijer-G convolution theorem for the second equation, we can derive the direct integral formula for
\Sigma^{\mathrm{p}}\to\varv_{\rm rot}^{2}
,

\varv_{\rm rot}^{2}(r)=\frac{2\pi G}{r}\!\int_{0}^{r}\!\mathrm{d}R\,R\Sigma^{\mathrm{p}}(R)\\
+4G\!\int_{r}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}R\left[\frac{R}{r}\sin^{-1}\Bigl(\frac{r}{R}\Bigr)-\frac{R}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}-r^{2}}}\right]\Sigma^{\mathrm{p}}(R),

which is verified directly. As for the question posed, we can compare equation (
VI.14
) to (
IV.5
). That is to say, if
\varv_{\rm rot}^{2}(r)=\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R=r)
, then the Mellin transform of
\Sigma(R)
and
\Sigma^{\mathrm{p}}(R)
should be related such that
\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(2-t)\mathfrak{M}[\Sigma](t)=\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-t)\mathfrak{M}[\Sigma^{\mathrm{p}}](t)
.

For the disk surface density of equation (
VI.1
), its mid-plane rotation curve given in equation (
VI.2
) is identical to the one due to the spherical profile:

\begin{split}\rho(r)&=\frac{3\Gamma(a+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(b)}{2\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b+\frac{1}{2})}\frac{\Sigma_{0}}{R_{0}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 52,a+\frac 12}{b+\frac 12};-\frac{r^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right)\\
&=\frac{2\Gamma(b)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(a)}\frac{\Sigma_{0}}{R_{0}}G^{1,2}_{2,2}\!\left(\frac{r^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-\frac 32,\frac 12-a}{0,\frac 12-b}\right),\end{split}

which projects to the column density of

\frac{\Sigma^{\mathrm{p}}(R)}{\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{2\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)}G^{1,2}_{2,2}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-1,1-a}{0,1-b}\right)=2\,{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{2,a}{b};-\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\right).

If
a=\frac{3}{2}
, the functional form of the projected column density also belongs to the same family as equation (
VI.1
) with
a=2
(the scale factor of
2
accounting for the mass difference; i.e. the spherical profile is twice as massive), which is indeed the case for the
Kalnajs 1976
isochrone disk.

VI.4
The disks with asymptotically flat rotation curves

We find equation (
VI.2
) with
a=\frac{1}{2}
tends to a finite non-zero limit as
R\to\infty
; that is,

\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{GR_{0}\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{4\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(b-\frac{1}{2})}=\varv_{\infty}^{2}.

Since the flat rotation curve implies the logarithmic divergence of the potential, the integral for
\Phi(\bm{r})
in equation (
VI.5
) does not converge. Instead, the zero-point-shifted potential is found similarly as equations (
II.5
); that is,

\begin{split}\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}&=\int_{R_{0}}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}h\left(1-\frac{R_{0}^{2}}{h^{2}}\right)^{b-\frac{3}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{h}-\frac{1}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+h)^{2}}}\right)\\
&=2\!\int_{R_{0}+r_{+}}^{\infty}\frac{|z|x+r^{2}}{x+|z|}\frac{\mathcal{Q}^{b-3/2}\mathrm{d}x}{(x^{2}-r^{2})^{2b-2}}\end{split}

(VI.15)

which converges if
b>a=\frac{1}{2}
. The last line utilizes the same change of the integration variable as equations (
VI.6
) with
\mathcal{Q}
defined there, and is in a manifest form of the Carlson-R, although the general result is of a minimal practical interest.

If
b-\frac{3}{2}
is an integer,
the integrand is a rational function of
x
and equation (
VI.15
) is expressible via elementary functions up to logarithms or inverse hyperbolic functions: e.g.,

\begin{split}\frac{\Phi}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}&=f_{0}=\ln\biggl(\frac{1+|\zeta|+\tau_{+}}{2}\biggr)\qquad(b=\tfrac{3}{2});\\
\frac{\Phi}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}&=f_{0}+\frac{f_{1}+\tau_{+}-1}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\qquad(b=\tfrac{5}{2}),\end{split}

where
\sigma
and
\tau_{+}
are as defined for equation (
VI.12
) and

f_{1}=-\frac{2|\zeta|}{\sigma}\tanh^{-1}\biggl(\frac{\sigma}{1+\tau_{+}}\biggr).

The explicit expression for the potential quickly becomes rather complicated with increasing
b
; for instance, if
b=\frac{7}{2}

\frac{\Phi}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}=f_{0}+\frac{4\sigma^{4}+3\varrho^{2}-2\zeta^{2}}{2\sigma^{6}}f_{1}+\frac{(4\varrho^{2}-11\zeta^{2})(\tau_{+}-1)}{6\sigma^{6}}\\
+\frac{5(2\sigma^{2}+|\zeta|)\tau_{+}}{6\sigma^{4}}-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{3}{4}.

The general expression for the corresponding surface density may be found from (where
m=b-\frac{3}{2}\in\set{1,2,3,\dotsc}
)

\Sigma(R)=\frac{\varv_{\infty}^{2}m!}{2\pi\,GR_{0}(\frac{3}{2})_{m}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,\frac 12}{m+\frac 32};-\varrho^{2}\right)\\
=\frac{\varv_{\infty}^{2}(1+\varrho^{2})^{m-\frac{1}{2}}}{2\pi GR_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{\varrho^{2m}}+\frac{(-1)^{m}}{(m-1)!}\,\biggl(\frac{\partial}{\partial\varrho^{2}}\biggr)^{m-1}\!\frac{\sinh^{-1}\!\varrho}{\varrho^{3}\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}}\right]

which further reduces to

\Sigma(R)=\frac{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi GR_{0}}\Biggl[\sum_{k=1}^{m}\!{{}_{4}F_{3}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,1,k+\frac 12,k-m}{\frac 32,k,k+1};1\right)\\
\times\binom{m}{k}\frac{(\frac{3}{2})_{k-1}}{(k-1)!}\frac{\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}}{\varrho^{2k}}\\
-\frac{(\frac{3}{2})_{m-1}}{(m-1)!}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-m,1-m}{\frac 12-m};-\varrho^{2}\right)\frac{\sinh^{-1}\!\varrho}{\varrho^{2m+1}}\Biggr].

The expression for the rotation curve is relatively simpler:

\begin{split}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)&=\frac{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}{2(m+1)}\frac{\varrho^{2}}{1+\varrho^{2}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{m+\frac 12,1}{m+2};\frac{\varrho^{2}}{1+\varrho^{2}}\right)\\
&=m!\varv_{\infty}^{2}\left[\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{1}{(m-k)!(\frac{1}{2})_{k}}\frac{1}{\varrho^{2k}}-\frac{(1+\varrho^{2})^{m-\frac{1}{2}}}{(\frac{1}{2})_{m}\,\varrho^{2m}}\right],\end{split}

while the vertical force for
m\in\set{1,2,3,\dotsc}
is in the form of

\varg(z)=\frac{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}{R_{0}}\left[\frac{2m(\zeta^{2}-1)^{m-1}}{\zeta^{2m+1}}\ln(1+\zeta)+\frac{p_{\varg}(\zeta)}{\zeta^{2m}}\right],

where
p_{\varg}(\zeta)
is a
(2m-1)
-th polynomial of
\zeta
and
\varg(z)
falls off asymptotically like
\varg(z)\simeq\varv_{\infty}^{2}/z
as
z\to\infty
independent of
b
. The resulting specific results for
b=\frac{5}{2}
(
m=1
) are

\frac{\Sigma(R)}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}=\frac{\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}-\hat{S}}{2\pi GR_{0}\,\varrho^{2}};\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}=1-\frac{2}{1+\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}};

and
p_{\varg}(\zeta)=\zeta-2
, where
\hat{S}=\varrho^{-1}\sinh^{-1}\!\varrho
. For
b=\frac{7}{2}
(
m=2
), we have
p_{\varg}(\zeta)=\zeta^{3}-(8/3)\zeta^{2}-2\zeta+4
and

\begin{split}\frac{\Sigma(R)}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}&=\frac{(3+2\varrho^{2})\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}-(3+4\varrho^{2})\hat{S}}{4\pi GR_{0}\,\varrho^{4}};\\
\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}&=1-\frac{4}{3}\frac{3+4\varrho^{2}}{2+3\varrho^{2}+2(1+\varrho^{2})^{3/2}}.\end{split}

The
b=\frac{3}{2}
(
m=0
) case is simply the cored Mestel disk.

VI.4.1
the potential with elliptic integrals of the third kind

Let us observe that equation (
VI.15
) is also reducible to

\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}=2\!\int_{R_{0}+r_{+}}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\!\sqrt{\mathcal{Q}}}\left(|z|+\frac{R^{2}}{x+|z|}\right)\left[1-\frac{4R_{0}^{2}(x+|z|)^{2}}{(x^{2}-r^{2})^{2}}\right]^{b-1};

Given that
\mathcal{Q}
is a quartic polynomial of
x
, if
b
is a positive integer, this is recognized as an elliptic integral of the third kind, which requires an additional basis (
R_{J}
or
\Pi
) besides those introduced earlier in Sect.
V.5
to be written down.

If
b=1
, the change of variable
u=2L_{+}M_{+}r_{+}/(x-R_{0}-r_{+})
in equation (
VI.15
) then results in

\begin{split}\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\!\sqrt{\mathcal{R}}}\left[\tau_{+}-1-\frac{2\tau_{+}(\tau_{+}-|\zeta|-1)}{u+2\tau_{+}}\right]\\
&=2(\tau_{+}-1)\,R_{F}(M_{+}\tau_{+},L_{+}M_{+},L_{+}\tau_{+})\\
&\phantom{=2(\tau_{+}}-\tfrac{4}{3}\tau_{+}L_{-}M_{-}\,R_{J}(M_{+}\tau_{+},L_{+}M_{+},L_{+}\tau_{+};2\tau_{+}),\end{split}

where
(L_{\pm},M_{\pm},\tau_{\pm})=(\lambda^{1/2}\pm 1,1\pm\mu^{1/2},\lambda^{1/2}\pm\mu^{1/2})
and
\mathcal{R}=(u+M_{+}\tau_{+})(u+L_{+}M_{+})(u+L_{+}\tau_{+})
. Here also note that
(M_{+}\tau_{+},L_{+}M_{+},L_{+}\tau_{+})=(\mu+\Xi,1+\Xi,\lambda+\Xi)
with
\Xi=\lambda^{1/2}+\mu^{1/2}+\lambda^{1/2}\mu^{1/2}
, and so the duplication formula of elliptic integrals (see e.g.,
Olver et al. 2010
, § 19.26(iii)) indicates
2R_{F}(M_{+}\tau_{+},L_{+}M_{+},L_{+}\tau_{+})=R_{F}(\mu,1,\lambda)
. In fact, also utilizing the duplication formula for
R_{J}
, one obtains

\begin{split}\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}&=(\tau_{+}-1)\,R_{F}-\tfrac{2}{3}\tau_{+}L_{-}M_{-}\,R_{J}(\mu,1,\lambda;\nu_{0})+\ln\nu_{0}\\
&=(1-\tau_{-})\,R_{F}+\tfrac{2}{3}\tau_{-}L_{-}M_{+}\,R_{J}(\mu,1,\lambda;\nu_{1})+\ln\nu_{1},\end{split}

where
R_{F}=R_{F}(\mu,1,\lambda)
with

\begin{split}\nu_{0}&=\tau_{+}-|\zeta|=\lambda^{1/2}+\mu^{1/2}-\lambda^{1/2}\mu^{1/2}=L_{-}M_{-}+1;\\
\nu_{1}&=|\zeta|+\tau_{-}=\lambda^{1/2}\mu^{1/2}+\lambda^{1/2}-\mu^{1/2}=L_{-}M_{+}+1.\end{split}

Notice that these expressions are naturally expressed in the prolate spheroidal coordinates
(\lambda,\mu)
defined in Sect.
V.2
. However, “simpler” expressions in terms of the same elliptic coordinates also exist. In particular, equation (
VI.15
) following the same variable change as equation (
VI.7
) results in

\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}y\,\frac{\mathcal{I}}{y}\left(\frac{\!\sqrt{\lambda(y+\lambda)}+\!\sqrt{\mu(y+\mu)}}{y+\lambda+\mu}\right)^{2(b-1)}

(VI.16)

where

\mathcal{I}=\frac{\!\sqrt{\lambda}}{\!\sqrt{y+\lambda}}+\frac{\!\sqrt{\mu}}{\!\sqrt{y+\mu}}-\frac{1}{\!\sqrt{y+1}}-\frac{\!\sqrt{\lambda\mu}}{\!\sqrt{(y+\mu)(y+1)(y+\lambda)}}.

Here
\mathcal{I}/y
is indeterminate (
0/0
) as
y\to 0
, and an obvious reduction to the Carlson-R also yields an indeterminate (
\infty-\infty
) expression. To find a properly convergent expression, one needs to employ the parameter change for the elliptic integral of the third kind (e.g.,
Olver et al. 2010
, § 19.7(iii) and 19.21(iii)) to cancel off the logarithmic singularities. Specifically, if
b=1
,

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0^{+}}\left[\ln\frac{2\!\sqrt{\lambda\mu}}{\!\sqrt{\epsilon}}-\frac{\!\sqrt{\lambda\mu}}{3}R_{J}(\epsilon+\mu,\epsilon+1,\epsilon+\lambda;\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle=|\zeta|\left[R_{F}(\mu,1,\lambda)-\frac{\varrho^{2}}{3}R_{J}(\mu,1,\lambda;\vartheta)\right]+\frac{\ln\vartheta}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\!\sqrt{\lambda}}{\!\sqrt{\mu}}\frac{\mathrm{F}(\varphi,\kappa)-(1-\mu)\,\Pi(\varphi,\varpi^{2},\kappa)}{\!\sqrt{\lambda-\mu}}+\ln\!\sqrt{\lambda+\mu-\lambda\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{\!\sqrt{\mu}\,\Pi(\varphi,\omega^{2},\kappa)}{\!\sqrt{\lambda}\sqrt{\lambda-\mu}}+\ln\!\sqrt{\lambda\mu+\lambda-\mu}

(VI.17)

where
\vartheta=\lambda+\mu-\lambda\mu=1+\varrho^{2}
and

\kappa^{2}=\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda-\mu},\hskip 10.00002pt\cos^{2}\!\varphi=\frac{\mu}{\lambda},\hskip 10.00002pt\varpi^{2}=\frac{(\lambda-1)\mu}{\lambda-\mu},\hskip 10.00002pt\omega^{2}=\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda}.

Note
0\leq\varpi^{2},\omega^{2}\leq\kappa^{2}
, and the arguments
(\varphi,\kappa)
are the same as defined for equation (
V.30
). By contrast, the corresponding potential for
R=0
or
z=0
is much simpler;
\Phi(R,0)=\varv_{\infty}^{2}\ln\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}
and
\Phi(0,z)=\varv_{\infty}^{2}|\zeta|\,R_{C}(\zeta^{2};1)
, where
R_{C}(x;y)=R_{F}(x,y,y)
is an elementary function such that

R_{C}(\zeta^{2};1)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{\cos^{-1}\!|\zeta|}{\sqrt{1-\zeta^{2}}}&(|\zeta|<1)\\
1&(|\zeta|=1)\\
\dfrac{\cosh^{-1}\!|\zeta|}{\sqrt{\zeta^{2}-1}}&(|\zeta|>1)\end{cases}.

(VI.18)

Hence the resulting rotation curve and the vertical force are

\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}=\frac{\varrho^{2}}{1+\varrho^{2}};\hskip 10.00002pt\varg(z)=\frac{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}{R_{0}}\frac{R_{C}(\zeta^{2};1)-\zeta}{1-\zeta^{2}}.

(VI.19)

Here if
z=R_{0}
(
\zeta=1
), then
\varg(R_{0})=(2\varv_{\infty}^{2})/(3R_{0})
. Interestingly,
\Phi(R,0)
and
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
of this model,
(a,b)=(\frac{1}{2},1)
, are the same as the so-called logarithmic potential of Binney, although the disk surface density generating this is given by

\begin{split}\Sigma(R)&=\frac{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}{4GR_{0}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,\frac 12}{1};-\varrho^{2}\right)\\
&=\frac{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi GR_{0}}\left[R_{F}(0,1,1+\varrho^{2})-\frac{\varrho^{2}}{3}R_{D}(0,1;1+\varrho^{2})\right]\\
&=\frac{\varv_{\infty}^{2}\mathbf{E}(\kappa_{0})}{2\pi G(R^{2}+R_{0}^{2})^{1/2}};\quad\left(\kappa_{0}^{2}=\frac{\varrho^{2}}{1+\varrho^{2}}=\frac{R^{2}}{R^{2}+R_{0}^{2}}\right).\end{split}

For other integer values of
b
, the surface density profile can be discovered using the differential recursion, namely

\Sigma(R)=\frac{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi GR_{0}}\frac{(1+\varrho^{2})^{b-2}}{\bigl(-\frac{1}{2}\bigr)_{b-1}}\,\biggl(-\frac{\partial}{\partial\varrho^{2}}\biggr)^{b-1}\Bigl[\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}\,\mathbf{E}(\kappa_{0})\Bigr],

whereas it is also possible to find the general expressions for

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}=&\frac{1}{2b-1}\frac{\varrho^{2}}{1+\varrho^{2}}\left[1-\sum_{k=0}^{b-2}\frac{(\frac{1}{2}-b)_{k}}{(2-b)_{k}}\left(\frac{1+\varrho^{2}}{\varrho^{2}}\right)^{k}\right]\\
&+\frac{(\frac{3}{2})_{b-2}}{(b-2)!}\left(\frac{1+\varrho^{2}}{\varrho^{2}}\right)^{b-2}\left(1-\frac{\tan^{-1}\!\varrho}{\varrho}\right);\end{split}

\displaystyle\varg(z)=\frac{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}{R_{0}}\left[\frac{(2b-3)(\zeta^{2}-1)^{b-2}}{\zeta^{2b-2}}R_{C}(\zeta^{2};1)+\frac{q_{\varg}(\zeta)}{\zeta^{2b-2}}\right].

where
q_{\varg}(\zeta)
is another
(2b-3)
-th polynomial of
\zeta
except
b=1
, for which
q_{\varg}(\zeta)=\zeta/(\zeta^{2}-1)
. For
b=2
, these result in

\begin{split}\Sigma(R)&=\frac{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi GR_{0}\varrho^{2}}\left[\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}\,\mathbf{E}(\kappa_{0})-\frac{\mathbf{K}(\kappa_{0})}{\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}}\right];\\
\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}&=1-\frac{\tan^{-1}\!\varrho}{\varrho};\quad\frac{\varg(z)}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}=\frac{R_{C}(\zeta^{2};1)+\zeta-\frac{\pi}{2}}{R_{0}\zeta^{2}},\end{split}

while corresponding potential is found from equation (
VI.16
):

\begin{split}\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}&=\frac{\Phi^{b=1}}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}-\frac{\sqrt{\lambda\mu}}{3}R_{J}(\mu,1,\lambda;\lambda+\mu)+\frac{\tan^{-1}\!\sigma}{\sigma}-1\\
&=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{\sqrt{\mu}}\frac{\Pi(\varphi,\tilde{\varpi}^{2},\kappa)-(1-\mu)\,\Pi(\varphi,\varpi^{2},\kappa)}{\sqrt{\lambda-\mu}}-1\\
&\qquad+\frac{\ln(\lambda+\mu-\lambda\mu)}{2}+\frac{\tan^{-1}\!\sigma}{\sigma}\\
&=\frac{\mu\,\Pi(\varphi,\omega^{2},\kappa)-\lambda\,\Pi(\varphi,\tilde{\omega}^{2},\kappa)}{\sqrt{\lambda\mu(\lambda-\mu)}}-1\\
&\qquad+\frac{\ln(\lambda\mu+\lambda-\mu)}{2}+\frac{1}{\sigma}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}+\tan^{-1}\!\frac{\lambda-1}{\sigma}\right)\end{split}

where
\Phi^{b=1}
is the potential for the
b=1
case given in equation (
VI.17
) , while
\sigma^{2}=\varrho^{2}+\zeta^{2}=\lambda+\mu-1
and

\tilde{\varpi}^{2}=-\frac{\mu}{\lambda-\mu},\hskip 10.00002pt\tilde{\omega}^{2}=-\frac{\lambda-1}{\mu}.

(VI.20)

The specialized results for
R=0
or
z=0
are given by

\begin{split}\frac{\Phi(R,0)}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}&=\ln\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}+\frac{\tan^{-1}\!\varrho}{\varrho}-1;\\
\frac{\Phi(0,z)}{\varv_{\infty}^{2}}&=\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}+(\zeta^{2}-1)\,R_{C}(\zeta^{2};1)}{|\zeta|}-1,\end{split}

which are also consistent with the earlier expression for
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)=R\,\mathrm{d}\Phi(R,0)/\mathrm{d}R
and
\varg(z)=\mathrm{d}\Phi(0,z)/\mathrm{d}z
.

VII
The extended Evans–de Zeeuw disk families:
the beta distribution weights

As shown in Appendix
B.2
, all Meijer-G functions of
G^{m,n}_{2,2}
(including
G^{1,2}_{2,2}
in eq.
IV.14
) are reducible to
{{}_{2}F_{1}}
. Hence, from equations (
IV.9
)–(
IV.11
), if we choose the
1
weight to be in the form of
G^{m,n}_{1,1}
(which are actually just geometric series; see App.
B.2
), both
\Sigma(R)
and
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
are expressible as
{{}_{2}F_{1}}
. As shown in equation (
VI.4
), the
1
weight for the QKMd is given by
G^{0,1}_{1,1}
. In the next two chapters, we shall consider the remaining two cases,
G^{1,0}_{1,1}
and
G^{1,1}_{1,1}
for the
1
weight.

First suppose that the
1
weight is given by

\begin{split}\Lambda(h)&=\frac{2M_{\mathrm{t}}}{R_{0}}\frac{\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(c)}\,G^{1,0}_{1,1}\!\left(\frac{h^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{b-\frac 12}{c-\frac 12}\right)\\
&=\frac{2M_{\mathrm{t}}}{R_{0}^{2b-2}}\frac{\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(c)}\frac{h^{2c-1}(R_{0}^{2}-h^{2})^{b-c-1}}{\Gamma(b-c)}\Theta(R_{0}-h)\end{split}

(VII.1)

with
b>c>0
. Then equations (
IV.9
)–(
IV.11
) indicate that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Sigma(R)&=\frac{M_{\mathrm{t}}}{R_{0}^{2}}\frac{\Gamma(b)}{\pi^{3/2}\Gamma(c)}G^{2,1}_{2,2}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-\frac 12,b-1}{0,c-1}\right)\\
&=\frac{\Gamma(b)\Gamma(c+\frac{1}{2})}{2\pi\Gamma(b+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(c)}\frac{M_{\mathrm{t}}R_{0}}{R^{3}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,c+\frac 12}{b+\frac 12};-\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}\right);\end{split}

(VII.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)&=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{R_{0}}\frac{2\Gamma(b)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(c)}G^{2,1}_{2,2}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,b-\frac 12}{1,c-\frac 12}\right)\\
&=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{R}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,c}{b};-\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}\right),\end{split}

(VII.3)

where
\varrho=R/R_{0}
with the
b=c
limit being the Kuzmin disk. These behave like
\Sigma\sim R^{-3}
and
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\sim R^{-1}
as
R\to\infty
. All disks in this family with
c>0
have a finite total mass
M_{\mathrm{t}}
with the enclosed mass profile given by

\begin{split}\frac{M(R)}{M_{\mathrm{t}}}&=\frac{\Gamma(b)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(c)}G^{2,2}_{3,3}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,\frac 12;b}{1,c;0}\right)\\
&=1-\frac{\Gamma(b)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(c)}G^{2,1}_{2,2}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,b}{0,c}\right)\\
&=1-\frac{\Gamma(b)\Gamma(c+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(b+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(c)}\frac{1}{\varrho}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,c+\frac 12}{b+\frac 12};-\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}\right)\end{split}

With the weight in equation (
VII.1
), the potential of the family is written down as a real quadrature (
\varrho=R/R_{0}
and
\zeta=z/R_{0}
);

\Phi(R,z)=-\frac{2GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{R_{0}}\frac{\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(b-c)\Gamma(c)}\!\int_{0}^{1}\!\frac{t^{2c-1}(1-t^{2})^{b-c-1}\mathrm{d}t}{\!\sqrt{\varrho^{2}+(|\zeta|+t)^{2}}}.

(VII.4)

VII.1
The dual KMT disks

The surface density of the model with
b=1
results in

\frac{\Sigma}{M_{\mathrm{t}}}=\frac{\Gamma(c+\frac{1}{2})}{R_{0}^{2}\pi^{3/2}\Gamma(c)}\frac{\varrho^{2c-2}}{(1+\varrho^{2})^{c+1/2}}

(VII.5)

with the rotation curve and the vertical force given by

\begin{split}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)&=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{R}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,c}{1};-\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}\right);\\
\varg(z)&=\frac{\Gamma(c+\frac{1}{2})}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(c+2)}\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{z^{2}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{3,2c}{c+2};\frac{\zeta-1}{2\zeta}\right).\end{split}

Here the model with
0<c<b=1
possesses a central density cusp,
\sim R^{-2(1-c)}
as
R\to 0
. If
c>b=1
on the other hand, the weight in equation (
VII.1
) is not proper and equation (
VII.4
) does not converge; that is to say, the surface density of equation (
VII.5
) with
c>1
is not expressible as the Stieltjes transform. The corresponding disks are mostly of a formal interest given not only that
\Sigma\to 0
as
R\to 0
but also that
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)<0
for a sufficiently small
R
for such models.

Nevertheless equation (
VII.5
) obeys the differential recursion similar to equation (
V.8
), namely
(\partial/\partial x)[R_{0}^{2c}\Sigma(R;c,b=1)]=-cR_{0}^{2c+2}\Sigma(R;c+1,b=1)
where
x=R_{0}^{-2}
. Consequently the potential for any model with
b=1
and
c>0
may be obtained using the fractional calculus analogous to the KMTd in Sect.
V.1
. In general, changing the integration variable in equation (
V.19
) via
t\to(z^{2}/R_{0}^{2})t^{-1}
leads to

\frac{1}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(z+R_{0})^{2}}}=\frac{z}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}t}{z^{2}+r^{2}t+R_{0}^{2}t(1+t)},

and it follows that equation (
VII.4
) may be recast to be

\Phi(\bm{r})=-\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}\Gamma(b)|z|}{\pi\Gamma(c)R_{0}^{2(b-1)}}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{\!\sqrt{t}}\,{{}_{0}^{+}I}_{R_{0}^{2}}^{b-c}\biggl[\frac{R_{0}^{2(c-1)}}{z^{2}+r^{2}t+R_{0}^{2}t(1+t)}\biggr]

(VII.6)

using the Riemann–Liouville integral
Erdélyi et al. 1954

{{}_{a}^{+}I}_{x}^{\eta}\,f(x)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma(\eta)}\int_{a}^{x}\!(x-y)^{\eta-1}f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y&(\eta>0)\\
f(x)&(\eta=0)\\
\displaystyle\biggl(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\biggr)^{\lceil\xi\rceil}{{}_{a}^{+}I}_{x}^{\lceil\xi\rceil-\xi}f&(\eta=-\xi<0)\end{cases},

which extends the formula to include
b\leq c
cases. Since we find
{{}_{0}^{+}I}_{x}^{-\xi}[x^{\xi}/(Ax+B)]=\Gamma(\xi+1)B^{\xi}(Ax+B)^{-\xi-1}
, the general expression for the potential due to
\Sigma
in equation (
VII.5
) from equation (
VII.6
) with
b=1
becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi(\bm{r})&=-\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}|z|}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{\!\sqrt{t}}\frac{(r^{2}t+z^{2})^{c-1}}{[t(t+1)R_{0}^{2}+r^{2}t+z^{2}]^{c}}\\
&=-\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{\pi R_{0}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}t\,t^{c-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{(\lambda+\mu-1+t)^{c-1}}{[(\lambda+t)(\mu+t)]^{c}}\end{split}

(VII.7)

\displaystyle=-\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}\Gamma(c+\frac{1}{2})}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(c+1)R_{0}}R_{-\frac{1}{2}}(c,1-c,c;\mu,\lambda+\mu-1,\lambda).

Note that the
c=0
case results in
\Phi=-(GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0})(\lambda+\mu-1)^{-1/2}=-GM_{\mathrm{t}}r^{-1}
, that is, the potential of a point mass
M_{\mathrm{t}}
. Although equation (
VII.5
) with
c=0
is technically unintegrable, one may instead identify it with
\lim_{c\to 0}\varrho^{2c-2}/\Gamma(c)\to\deltaup(\varrho^{2})=R_{0}^{2}\deltaup(R^{2})
. Then the central point mass is indeed the formal limit corresponding to the
c=0
case. If
c
is a positive integer, the potential is expressible through elementary functions, but specific forms are easier to find through the differential recurrence relation instead; that is, for
c\in\set{1,2,3,\cdots}
,

\begin{split}\Phi(\bm{r})&=\frac{(-1)^{c}GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{(c-1)!R_{0}^{2c}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial(R_{0}^{-2})}\right)^{c-1}\frac{R_{0}^{2}}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+R_{0})^{2}}}\\
&=-\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{(c-1)!}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial R_{0}^{2}}\right)^{c-1}\frac{R_{0}^{2(c-1)}}{\!\sqrt{R^{2}+(|z|+R_{0})^{2}}}.\end{split}

For a half-integer value of
c
, the Carlson-R of equation (
VII.7
) is actually an elliptic integral. If
c=\frac{1}{2}
in particular,

\displaystyle\Sigma(R)

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{\mathrm{t}}R_{0}}{\pi^{2}R(R^{2}+R_{0}^{2})}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\

\displaystyle\Phi(\bm{r})

\displaystyle=-\frac{2GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{\pi R_{0}}R_{F}(\mu,\lambda+\mu-1,\lambda)=-\frac{2GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{\pi R_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{F}(\varphi,\tilde{\kappa})}{\sqrt{\lambda-\mu}};

\displaystyle\left(\text{where }\ \cos^{2}\!\varphi=\frac{\mu}{\lambda},\hskip 10.00002pt\tilde{\kappa}^{2}=\frac{1-\mu}{\lambda-\mu}\right),

(VII.8)

which is also obtained by changing the integration variable of equation (
VII.4
) similarly to equation (
VI.7
). Here
\varphi
is the same as equation (
V.30
) but
\tilde{\kappa}^{2}=1-\kappa^{2}
. The specialized results for
R=0
or
z=0
are then found to be

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi(R,0)}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\pi}R_{F}(0,\varrho^{2},1+\varrho^{2})=-\frac{2}{\pi}\frac{\mathbf{K}(\tilde{\kappa}_{0})}{\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}}

(VII.9)

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi(0,z)}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\pi}R_{C}(1;\zeta^{2})=-\frac{2}{\pi}\begin{cases}\dfrac{\mbox{sech}^{-1}|\zeta|}{\sqrt{1-\zeta^{2}}}&(|\zeta|<1)\\
1&(|\zeta|=1)\\
\dfrac{\sec^{-1}\!|\zeta|}{\sqrt{\zeta^{2}-1}}&(|\zeta|>1)\end{cases}

where
\tilde{\kappa}_{0}^{2}=(1+\varrho^{2})^{-1}
and
R_{C}(1;\zeta^{2})=|\zeta|^{-1}R_{C}(\zeta^{-2};1)
, while

\frac{R_{0}\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}=\frac{2}{\pi}\frac{\mathbf{E}(\tilde{\kappa}_{0})}{\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}};\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{R_{0}^{2}\varg(z)}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}=\frac{2}{\pi}\frac{\zeta R_{C}(1;\zeta^{2})-\zeta^{-1}}{\zeta^{2}-1}

with
R_{0}^{2}\varg(R_{0})/(GM_{\mathrm{t}})=4/(3\pi)
. The explicit expressions for
c\in\set{\frac{3}{2},\frac{5}{2},\frac{7}{2},\dotsc}
may then be obtained by similarly applying the differential recursion relation on the
c=\frac{1}{2}
results; that is,
(\partial/\partial x)^{m}(R_{0}^{2c}f_{c})=(-1)^{m}(c)_{m}R_{0}^{2(c+m)}f_{c+m}
with
x=R_{0}^{-2}
for any quantity
f_{c}
linear to
\Sigma
.

VII.2
The dual QKM disks

For arbitrary
b>c>0
, the pair of
\Sigma
in equation (
VII.2
) and
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
in equation (
VII.3
) behaves as
R\to 0
like

\frac{\Sigma}{M_{\mathrm{t}}/(\pi R_{0}^{2})}\simeq\begin{cases}\dfrac{b-1}{2(c-1)}&(c>1)\\
\displaystyle\frac{b-1}{2}\,\biggl(\ln\frac{4}{\varrho^{2}}-H_{b-2}-2\biggr)&(c=1)\\
\dfrac{\Gamma(b)\Gamma(c+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(1-c)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(b-c)\Gamma(c)\,\varrho^{2(1-c)}}&(c<1)\end{cases}

and

\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}\simeq\begin{cases}\dfrac{\Gamma(b)\Gamma(c-\frac{3}{2})}{\Gamma(b-\frac{3}{2})\Gamma(c)}\,\varrho^{2}&(c>\frac{3}{2})\\
\displaystyle\frac{2\Gamma(b)\,\varrho^{2}}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(b-\frac{3}{2})}\,\biggl(\ln\frac{4}{\varrho^{2}}-H_{b-\frac{5}{2}}-2\biggr)&(c=\frac{3}{2})\\
\dfrac{2\Gamma(b)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-c)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(b-c)}\,\varrho^{2c-1}&(c<\frac{3}{2})\end{cases},

where
H_{z}=\digamma(z+1)+\gammaup
is the generalized Harmonic number. In other words, either
\Sigma(R)
is cusped for
c\leq 1
or
\Sigma(0)
is constant for
c>1
, whereas
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)\to 0
as
R\to 0
if
c>\frac{1}{2}
. The
b>c=\frac{1}{2}
models possess the
R^{-1}
-cusp in
\Sigma
and
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(0)=2\Gamma(b)/[\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(b-\frac{1}{2})]
is finite. On the other hand, the vertical force resulting from the family is given by

\varg(z)=\frac{2GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{\pi R_{0}^{2}}\frac{\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(c)}G^{3,2}_{3,3}\!\left(\frac{z^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{0,-\frac 12,b-1}{\frac 12,0,c-1}\right)

which behaves like
\varg(z)\simeq GM_{\mathrm{t}}z^{-2}
as
z\to\infty
, and for
z\to 0
,

\frac{\varg(z)}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}^{2}}\simeq\begin{cases}\dfrac{b-1}{c-1}&(c>1)\\
(b-1)(\ln\zeta^{-2}-H_{b-2}-2)&(c=1)\\
\dfrac{2^{2c}\Gamma(c+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(2-2c)\Gamma(b)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(b-c)\,\zeta^{2(1-c)}}&(c<1)\end{cases}.

In principle, the integral of equation (
VII.4
) is reducible to the Carlson-R: that is, the change of the integration variable,
t\to\tilde{x}
given by
t=2(\sigma\tilde{x}+|\zeta|)/(\tilde{x}^{2}-1)
leads to

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2^{2c+1}\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(c)\Gamma(b-c)}\!\int_{\sigma+\tau_{+}}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\frac{(\sigma\tilde{x}+|\zeta|)^{2c-1}}{(\tilde{x}^{2}-1)^{2b-2}}\mathcal{S}^{b-c-1};

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=\left[(\tilde{x}-\sigma)^{2}-\tau_{+}^{2}\right]\left[(\tilde{x}+\sigma)^{2}-\tau_{-}^{2}\right],

(VII.10)

where
\sigma^{2}=\varrho^{2}+\zeta^{2}
and
\tau_{\pm}^{2}=\varrho^{2}+(|\zeta|\pm 1)^{2}
. This is reducible to elementary functions if
b-c
and
2c
are both integers, while it is an elliptic integral for an integer value of

2c
and a half-integer value of
b-c
.

The models with
b-c=1
and
c\in\set{\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2},2,\cdots}
may be seen as the formal dual of the Kalnajs–Mestel disk (cf. Sect.
VI.2
). Resolving the integral is then basically an exercise on the partial fraction decomposition, and in particular the potential of the
(b,c)=(\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2})
case results in

\frac{-\Phi(\bm{r})}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}=2\coth^{-1}(\tau_{+}+\sigma),

(VII.11)

with the
R=0
or
z=0
specializations being

\frac{-\Phi(R,0)}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}=\sinh^{-1}\biggl(\frac{R_{0}}{R}\biggr);\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{-\Phi(0,z)}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}=\ln\biggl(1+\frac{R_{0}}{|z|}\biggr).

We observe that this is identical to the potential of the Mestel-difference disk in equation (
II.10
) with
(c_{1},c_{2})=(R_{0},0)
. In fact, equations (
VII.2
–
VII.3
) with
(b,c)=(\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2})
result in

\Sigma(R)=\frac{M_{\mathrm{t}}}{2\pi R_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{R}-\frac{1}{(R^{2}+R_{0}^{2})^{1/2}}\right];\hskip 10.00002pt\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{(R^{2}+R_{0}^{2})^{1/2}},

whereas the vertical force profile is found to be

\varg(z)=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{z(z+R_{0})}=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{R_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{z}-\frac{1}{z+R_{0}}\right).

That is to say, this is the difference between the scale-free and cored Mestel disks of the same asymptotic circular speed.

Similarly we also find

\begin{split}\frac{-\Phi(\bm{r})}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}&=2\left(\tau_{+}-\sigma-|\zeta\Phi^{\rm MD}|\right);\\
\frac{-\Phi(\bm{r})}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}&=\frac{3}{2}\left[\tau_{+}-3|\zeta|(\tau_{+}-\sigma)+(2\zeta^{2}-\varrho^{2})|\Phi^{\rm MD}|\right];\\
\frac{-\Phi(\bm{r})}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}&=\frac{2}{3}\left[(2-5|\zeta|)\tau_{+}-(4\varrho^{2}-11\zeta^{2})(\tau_{+}-\sigma)\right]\\
&\qquad+2(3\varrho^{2}-2\zeta^{2})|\zeta\Phi^{\rm MD}|,\end{split}

for the models with
(b,c)=(2,1)
,
(\frac{5}{2},\frac{3}{2})
and
(3,2)
. Here
|\Phi^{\rm MD}|=2\coth^{-1}(\tau_{+}+\sigma)
is the rescaled equation (
VII.11
). These are generated by the surface density profiles given by

\displaystyle\frac{\Sigma(R)}{M_{\mathrm{t}}/(\pi R_{0}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\sinh^{-1}\frac{1}{\varrho}-\frac{1}{\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}};

\displaystyle(b,c)

\displaystyle=(2,1),

\displaystyle\frac{\Sigma(R)}{M_{\mathrm{t}}/(\pi R_{0}^{2})}

\displaystyle=3\left(\frac{1+2\varrho^{2}}{2\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}}-\varrho\right);

\displaystyle(b,c)

\displaystyle=(\tfrac{5}{2},\tfrac{3}{2}),

\displaystyle\frac{\Sigma(R)}{M_{\mathrm{t}}/(\pi R_{0}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+3\varrho^{2}}{\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}}-3\varrho^{2}\sinh^{-1}\frac{1}{\varrho};

\displaystyle(b,c)

\displaystyle=(3,2).

In general, equations (
VII.2
–
VII.3
) with
b-c=1
result in

\begin{split}\Sigma(R)&=\frac{M_{\mathrm{t}}}{\pi R^{2}}\frac{c}{2c+1}\frac{1}{\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{c,1}{c+\frac 32};-\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}\right);\\
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)&=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{(R^{2}+R_{0}^{2})^{1/2}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{c-\frac 12,1}{c+1};-\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}\right),\end{split}

and
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
of the model with
(b,c)=(n+1,n)
follows the same function of
R
as
R^{2}\Sigma(R)
with
(b,c)=(n+\frac{1}{2},n-\frac{1}{2})
.

On the other hand, the simplest potential expressible with elliptic integrals is provided in equation (
VII.8
) with
(b,c)=(1,\frac{1}{2})
, for which
\Sigma(R)
is elementary. In general, if
b
is an integer but
c
is a half-integer, then
\Sigma(R)
is an elementary function (which may involve
\cot^{-1}\varrho
) but
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
would require elliptic integrals to write down. By contrast, if
c
is an integer but
b
is a half-integer,
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)
is an elementary function but
\Sigma(R)
is an elliptic integral; for example, if
(b,c)=(\frac{3}{2},1)
,

\begin{split}\Sigma(R)&=\frac{M_{\mathrm{t}}}{2\pi R_{0}^{2}}\frac{\mathbf{K}(\tilde{\kappa}_{0})-\mathbf{E}(\tilde{\kappa}_{0})}{\!\sqrt{1+\varrho^{2}}};\\
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)&=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}R^{2}}{R^{2}+R_{0}^{2}}=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{R_{0}}\!\frac{\varrho}{1+\varrho^{2}},\\
\varg(z)&=\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{R_{0}^{2}}\frac{R_{C}(1;\zeta^{2})-1}{1-\zeta^{2}}\ \left(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle z=R_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\frac{GM_{\mathrm{t}}}{3R_{0}^{2}}\right).\end{split}

Although equation (
VII.10
) is in the form of the Carlson-R (and so it should be straightforward to express this with Carlson’s symmetric bases if it is indeed an elliptic integral),
the final expressions are easier to read if we introduce the elliptic coordinates
(\lambda,\mu)
again and consider the change of variable,
t=[\!\sqrt{\lambda(y+\mu)}-\!\sqrt{\mu(y+\lambda)}]/[\!\sqrt{\lambda(y+\lambda)}-\!\sqrt{\mu(y+\mu)}]
for equation (
VII.4
), which results in

\frac{-\Phi(R,z)}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}=\frac{\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(b-c)\Gamma(c)}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{\mathcal{F}^{2c-1}\mathcal{G}^{2-2b}\mathrm{d}y}{\!\sqrt{(y+\mu)(y+\sigma^{2})(y+\lambda)}}

where
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
are the same as in equation (
VI.7
). Also note that
0\leq\mu\leq\sigma^{2}=\lambda+\mu-1\leq\lambda
. If
(b,c)=(1,\frac{1}{2})
, this reproduces to the potential in equation (
VII.8
). For the
(b,c)=(\frac{3}{2},1)
case, it is straightforward to show that

\begin{split}\frac{-\Phi(\bm{r})}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}&=\cot^{-1}\sigma-\frac{|\zeta|}{3}R_{J}(\mu,\sigma^{2},\lambda;\lambda+\mu)\\
&=\frac{\pi}{2}-|\zeta|\left[R_{F}(\mu,\sigma^{2},\lambda)+\frac{\varrho^{2}}{3}R_{J}(\mu,\sigma^{2},\lambda;\lambda\mu)\right]\\
&=\cot^{-1}\sigma+\frac{\!\sqrt{\lambda}}{\!\sqrt{\mu}}\frac{\Pi(\varphi,\tilde{\varpi}^{2},\tilde{\kappa})-\mathrm{F}(\varphi,\tilde{\kappa})}{\!\sqrt{\lambda-\mu}}\\
&=\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{\!\sqrt{\mu}}{\!\sqrt{\lambda}}\frac{(\lambda-1)\Pi(\varphi,\hat{\varpi}^{2},\tilde{\kappa})+\mathrm{F}(\varphi,\tilde{\kappa})}{\!\sqrt{\lambda-\mu}}\\
&=\frac{\pi}{2}+\tan^{-1}\!\frac{1-\mu}{\sigma}-\frac{\!\sqrt{\lambda}}{\!\sqrt{\mu}}\frac{\Pi(\varphi,\hat{\omega}^{2},\tilde{\kappa})}{\!\sqrt{\lambda-\mu}}\end{split}

where
(\varphi,\tilde{\kappa})
is as defined in equation (
VII.8
) and

\tilde{\varpi}^{2}=-\frac{\mu}{\lambda-\mu},\hskip 10.00002pt\hat{\varpi}^{2}=\frac{\lambda(1-\mu)}{\lambda-\mu},\hskip 10.00002pt\hat{\omega}^{2}=-\frac{1-\mu}{\mu}.

Here
\tilde{\varpi}^{2}
is the same as equation (
VI.20
) but the transformed parameters
\hat{\varpi}^{2}
and
\hat{\omega}^{2}
are not. If
R=0
or
z=0
,

\frac{-\Phi(R,0)}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}=\cot^{-1}\varrho;\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{-\Phi(0,z)}{GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R_{0}}=\frac{\pi}{2}-|\zeta|\,R_{C}(1;\zeta^{2}),

which are consistent with both the rotation curve and the vertical force in the upper half-space.

VIII
The semi-infinite beta-prime weights

Suppose that the
1
weight is given by

\Lambda(h)=\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\Gamma(\beta+\gamma-1)}\,G^{1,1}_{1,1}\!\left(\frac{h^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32-\gamma}{\beta-\frac 12}\right)=\frac{\Lambda_{0}R_{0}^{2\gamma-1}h^{2\beta-1}}{(h^{2}+R_{0}^{2})^{\beta+\gamma-1}}.

Equation (
IV.10
) indicates the resulting surface density to be

\begin{split}\frac{\Sigma(R)}{\Lambda_{0}/R_{0}}&=\frac{1}{2\pi^{3/2}\Gamma(\beta+\gamma-1)}\,G^{2,2}_{2,2}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-\frac 12,1-\gamma}{0,\beta-1}\right)\\
&=\frac{\Gamma(\beta+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(\gamma)}{4\pi\Gamma(\beta+\gamma+\frac{1}{2})}\,{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,\gamma}{\beta+\gamma+\frac 12};{1-\varrho^{2}}\right),\end{split}

(VIII.1)

where
\varrho=R/R_{0}
, with the limiting behavior as
R\to 0
:

\frac{\Sigma(R)}{\Lambda_{0}/R_{0}}\simeq\begin{cases}\dfrac{\Gamma(\beta-1)\Gamma(\gamma)}{4\pi\Gamma(\beta+\gamma-1)}&(\beta>1)\\
\dfrac{1}{4\pi}\,\biggl(\ln\dfrac{4}{\varrho^{2}}-H_{\gamma-1}-2\biggr)&(\beta=1)\\
\dfrac{\Gamma(\beta+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(1-\beta)}{2\pi^{3/2}}\dfrac{1}{\varrho^{2(1-\beta)}}&(\beta<1)\end{cases},

and that as
R\to\infty
:

\frac{\Sigma(R)}{\Lambda_{0}/R_{0}}\simeq\begin{cases}\dfrac{\Gamma(\beta+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{3}{2})}{4\pi\Gamma(\beta+\gamma-1)}\dfrac{1}{\varrho^{3}}&(\gamma>\frac{3}{2})\\
\dfrac{1}{4\pi}\,\biggl[\ln(4\varrho^{2})-H_{\beta-\frac{3}{2}}-2\biggr]\,\dfrac{1}{\varrho^{3}}&(\gamma=\frac{3}{2})\\
\dfrac{\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)}{2\pi^{3/2}}\dfrac{1}{\varrho^{2\gamma}}&(\gamma<\frac{3}{2})\end{cases}.

Thus it is necessary
\beta>0
for
\Sigma(R)
to be integrable at the center – note
\Sigma(0)
is finite if
\beta>1
– and
\gamma>0
for it to be non-negative. Next the mass profile follows as

\frac{M(R)}{\Lambda_{0}R_{0}}=\frac{1}{2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\beta+\gamma-1)}\,G^{2,3}_{3,3}\!\left(\varrho^{2}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,\frac 12,2-\gamma}{1,\beta,0}\right),

which grows near
R\sim 0
like

\frac{M(R)}{\Lambda_{0}R_{0}}\simeq\begin{cases}\dfrac{\Gamma(\beta-1)\Gamma(\gamma)}{4\Gamma(\beta+\gamma-1)}\,\varrho^{2}&(\beta>1)\\
\dfrac{1}{4}\,\biggl(\ln\dfrac{4}{\varrho^{2}}-H_{\gamma-1}-1\biggr)\,\varrho^{2}&(\beta=1)\\
\dfrac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\beta)\Gamma(1-\beta)}{2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\beta}\,\varrho^{2\beta}&(\beta<1)\end{cases}.

On the other hand, as
R\to\infty
,

\frac{M(R)}{\Lambda_{0}R_{0}}\simeq\begin{cases}\dfrac{\Gamma(\beta)\Gamma(\gamma-1)}{2\Gamma(\beta+\gamma-1)}&(\gamma>1)\\
\ln\dfrac{\varrho}{2}-\dfrac{H_{\beta-1}}{2}&(\gamma=1)\\
\dfrac{\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)}{2(1-\gamma)\!\sqrt{\pi}}\,\varrho^{2(1-\gamma)}&(\gamma<1)\end{cases}.

(VIII.2)

Since
\Sigma(R)
for
\gamma>1
decays faster than
\sim R^{-2}
as
R\to\infty
, the corresponding disk has a finite total mass. The mass profiles of these finite mass cases are also expressible to be

\displaystyle\frac{M(R)}{M_{\mathrm{t}}}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{\!\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\beta)\Gamma(\gamma-1)}G^{2,2}_{2,2}\!\left(\varrho^{2}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,2-\gamma}{0,\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\Gamma(\beta+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(\beta+\gamma-1)}{\Gamma(\beta)\Gamma(\beta+\gamma-\frac{1}{2})}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,\gamma-1}{\beta+\gamma-\frac 12};{1-\varrho^{2}}\right),

where
M_{\mathrm{t}}
is the total mass of the disk whose numerical value is inferable from the
\gamma>1
case of equation (
VIII.2
).

The circular speed of these disks follows equation (
IV.11
);

\begin{split}\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{G\Lambda_{0}}&=\frac{1}{\!\sqrt{\pi}}\,G^{2,2}_{2,2}\!\left(\frac{R^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\vrule\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 12,\frac 32-\gamma}{1,\beta-\frac 12}\right)\\
&=\frac{\Gamma(\beta)\Gamma(\gamma+\frac{1}{2})}{2\Gamma(\beta+\gamma+\frac{1}{2})}\frac{1}{\varrho}\,{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac 32,\beta}{\beta+\gamma+\frac 12};1-\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}\right).\end{split}

(VIII.3)

which behaves asymptotically as
R\to\infty
like

\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{G\Lambda_{0}}=\begin{cases}\dfrac{\Gamma(\beta)\Gamma(\gamma-1)}{2\Gamma(\beta+\gamma-1)}\dfrac{1}{\varrho}&(\gamma>1)\\
\dfrac{1}{2}\,\biggl[\ln(4\varrho^{2})-H_{\beta-1}-2\biggr]\,\dfrac{1}{\varrho}&(\gamma=1)\\
\dfrac{\Gamma(\gamma+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(1-\gamma)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}}\dfrac{1}{\varrho^{2\gamma-1}}&(\gamma<1)\end{cases}.

Obviously, the rotation curve of a finite mass model approaches the Keplerian model,
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\simeq GM_{\mathrm{t}}/R
as
R\to\infty
. By contrast, the
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
model exhibits an asymptotically flat rotation curve with
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\to\varv_{\infty}^{2}=G\Lambda_{0}
. As for the behavior as
R\to 0
, we find
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}\to 0
if
\beta>1
. Specifically,

\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{G\Lambda_{0}}=\begin{cases}\dfrac{\Gamma(\beta-\frac{3}{2})\Gamma(\gamma+\frac{1}{2})}{2\Gamma(\beta+\gamma-1)}\,\varrho^{2}&(\beta>\frac{3}{2})\\
\dfrac{1}{2}\,\biggl(\ln\dfrac{4}{\varrho^{2}}-H_{\gamma-\frac{1}{2}}-2\biggr)\,\varrho^{2}&(\beta=\frac{3}{2})\\
\dfrac{\Gamma(\beta)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\beta)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}}\,\varrho^{2\beta-1}&(\beta<\frac{3}{2})\end{cases}.

From equation (
III.1
), the potential due to this disk
family is written as an integral quadrature
(
\varrho=R/R_{0}
and
\zeta=z/R_{0}
):

\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{G\Lambda_{0}}=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{2\beta-1}}{(1+t^{2})^{\beta+\gamma-1}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{\!\sqrt{\varrho^{2}+(|\zeta|+t)^{2}}},

(VIII.4)

which converges if
\beta>0
and
\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
.
Alternatively, provided
\beta>\frac{1}{2}
and
\gamma>0
,
the zero-point-shifted potential for the model with a non-decaying
rotation curve may be set up as

\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{G\Lambda_{0}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}t\,\frac{t^{2\beta-2}}{(t^{2}+1)^{\beta+\gamma-1}}\frac{\sqrt{\varrho^{2}+(|\zeta|+t)^{2}}-t}{\!\sqrt{\varrho^{2}+(|\zeta|+t)^{2}}}.

(VIII.5)

VIII.1
Scale-free disks

If
\beta+\gamma=1
, equation (
VIII.1
) results in

\frac{\Sigma(R)}{\Lambda_{0}/R_{0}}=\frac{\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)}{2\pi^{3/2}\varrho^{2\gamma}};\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{M(R)}{\Lambda_{0}R_{0}}=\frac{\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)}{2\!\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\varrho^{2(1-\gamma)}}{1-\gamma},

which follow strict power-laws (also
\Lambda\propto\varrho^{1-2\gamma}
and
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\propto\varrho^{1-2\gamma}
). In other words, the potential of the scale-free disk,
\Sigma(R)\propto R^{-2\gamma}
(with
0<\gamma<1
) may be examined through the current model with
\beta=1-\gamma
. Let us consider the scale-free disk with the enclosed mass growing with the radius
R
like

\frac{M(R)}{M_{0}}=\biggl(\frac{R}{R_{0}}\biggr)^{2-2\gamma}=\varrho^{2(1-\gamma)}

where
R_{0}
is a fiducial length and
M_{0}=M(R_{0})
is the scale factor, which relates to the constant
\Lambda_{0}
via
2\!\sqrt{\pi}\,(1-\gamma)M_{0}=\Lambda_{0}R_{0}\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)
. The corresponding surface density is then extended to include the
\gamma=1
model (i.e. the point mass):

\displaystyle\Sigma(R)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\frac{\mathrm{d}M(R)}{\mathrm{d}R}=\frac{M_{0}}{\pi R_{0}^{2}}\frac{1-\gamma}{\varrho^{2\gamma}}

\displaystyle(\gamma

\displaystyle<1);

\displaystyle\Sigma(R)

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{0}}{\pi R_{0}^{2}}\deltaup(\varrho^{2})=\frac{M_{0}}{\pi}\deltaup(R^{2})=\frac{M_{0}}{\pi}\frac{\deltaup(R)}{2R}

\displaystyle(\gamma

\displaystyle=1).

Equation (
VIII.3
) indicates that the rotation curve is like

\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)=C_{\gamma}\frac{GM_{0}}{R_{0}}\frac{1}{\varrho^{2\gamma-1}};\hskip 10.00002ptC_{\gamma}=\frac{2\Gamma(\gamma+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(2-\gamma)}{\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)},

while the direct integration (eq.
IV.13
) results in

\varg(z)=D_{\gamma}\frac{GM_{0}}{R_{0}^{2}}\frac{1}{\zeta^{\gamma}};\hskip 10.00002ptD_{\gamma}=\frac{2\Gamma(\gamma+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(2-\gamma)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}}.

(VIII.6)

Both results are valid for
-\frac{1}{2}<\gamma\leq 1
. If
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, then
1<C_{\gamma}\leq C_{\frac{3}{4}}(\approx 1.09422)
and
D_{\frac{3}{4}}(\approx 0.927037)\leq D_{\gamma}<1
, while
C_{\gamma}=D_{\gamma}=1
for
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
or
\gamma=1
, and
0<C_{\gamma}<1
and
1<D_{\gamma}<2
for
0<\gamma<\frac{1}{2}
.
The
\gamma=1
case corresponds to the point mass
M_{0}
at the center, and the results are simply the Kepler rotation curve,
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=GM_{0}/R
and the inverse square force
\varg=GM_{0}/z^{2}
. The
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
case (
\Sigma\propto R^{-1}
), for which
\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=GM(R)/R=GM_{0}/R_{0}=\varv_{0}^{2}
and
\varg=\varv_{0}^{2}/z
, is the scale-free Mestel disk (see Sect.
II.1
), which is essentially the disk analogue of a singular isothermal sphere. The
\gamma=0
case (for which
C_{\gamma}=0
and
D_{\gamma}=2
) is an infinite uniform disk: there should be no net gravitational force within the disk thanks to the translation symmetry and the vertical force is independent of the height
(
Routh 1892
, § 22)
. If
-\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<0
, we find
C_{\gamma}<0
, which indicates that the total gravity due to such disks would act outward in the centrifugal direction. If
\gamma\leq-\frac{1}{2}
, the force due to matters at infinity dominates, and so all the integrals accounting for the gravitational force diverge. Although the formal mathematical treatment is still possible, an infinite disk is an artificial construction and so this is not in fact a physical problem and shall not be discussed further.

The potential generated by the scale-free disk with
\Sigma\propto R^{-2\gamma}
, according to equation (
VIII.4
), is given by

\frac{-\Phi(\bm{r})}{G\Lambda_{0}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{1-\gamma}\mathrm{d}t}{\!\sqrt{\varrho^{2}+(|\zeta|+t)^{2}}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}x\frac{x^{1-\gamma}(x+\sigma)^{1-\gamma}}{(x+x_{0})^{2-\gamma}},

(VIII.7)

where
2x_{0}=\sigma+|\zeta|
and
\sigma^{2}=\varrho^{2}+\zeta^{2}
, which converges if
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
. The second expression follows changing the variable;
t=x(x+\sigma)/(x+x_{0})
and
t+|\zeta|=x+x_{0}-\varrho^{2}/[4(x+x_{0})]
. The integral in equation (
VIII.7
) then results in

\frac{\Phi(\bm{r})}{GM_{0}/R_{0}}=-\frac{D_{\gamma}}{2\gamma-1}R_{1-2\gamma}\!\left(2-2\gamma,2\gamma-1;\frac{|\zeta|+\sigma}{2},\sigma\right)\\
=-\frac{D_{\gamma}}{2\gamma-1}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2\gamma-1}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\!\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{2\gamma-1,2-2\gamma}{1};\frac{\sigma-|\zeta|}{2\sigma}\right),

(VIII.8)

where
D_{\gamma}
is defined in equation (
VIII.6
). Here the
{{}_{2}F_{1}}
-function is equivalent to the Legendre-P function,
P_{1-2\gamma}(x)=P_{2\gamma-2}(x)={{}_{2}F_{1}}(2\gamma-1,2-2\gamma;1;y)
with
2y=1-x
, and so

\Phi(\bm{r})=-\frac{\Gamma(\gamma-\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(2-\gamma)}{\!\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{GM_{0}}{R_{0}}\frac{P_{1-2\gamma}(|\cos\theta|)}{(r/R_{0})^{2\gamma-1}},

(VIII.9)

where
\cos\theta=z/r=\zeta/\sigma
(i.e.
\theta
is the usual zenith angle in the spherical polar coordinate). Furthermore,

\Phi(R,z=0)=-\frac{\varv_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(R)}{2\gamma-1};\hskip 10.00002pt\Phi(R=0,z)=-\frac{z\varg(z)}{2\gamma-1},

given
P_{1-2\gamma}(1)=1
and
\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)P_{1-2\gamma}(0)=\!\sqrt{\pi}
. Let us observe that
f=-Ar^{\eta}P_{\eta}(\cos\theta)
with a fixed
\eta
is the axisymmetric solution of the Laplace equation
\nabla^{2}\Phi=0
, obtainable via the separation of variables in the spherical polar coordinate
(r,\theta,\phi)
. Unlike the usual spherical harmonics basis, we concern the behavior of the solution limited in the upper half-space and so
\eta
need not necessarily be an integer, for the singular behavior at
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
is irrelevant. In other words, equation (
VIII.9
) satisfies the Laplace equation for
z\neq 0
, and so corresponds to the potential due to an infinitesimally thin disk.

Equations (
VIII.8
-
VIII.9
) are actually well-defined for any
\gamma\leq 1
unless
\frac{1}{2}-\gamma
is a non-negative integer. In fact, they correspond to the true potential of the scale-free disk,
\Sigma\propto R^{-2\gamma}
with
-\frac{1}{2}<\gamma\leq 1
and
\gamma\neq\frac{1}{2}
, for they satisfy the Poisson equation. The zero-point of these expressions for
-\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<\frac{1}{2}
is at the origin such that
\Phi(R=0,z=0)=0
. It is also easy to observe that equation (
VIII.9
) for
\gamma=1
(cf.
P_{0}(x)=P_{-1}(x)=1
) reduces to the Kepler potential
\Phi=-GM_{0}/r
, while the
\gamma=0
case (cf.
P_{1}(x)=R_{-2}(x)=x
) results in
\Phi=2\pi G\Sigma_{0}|z|
where
\Sigma_{0}=M/(\pi R_{0}^{2})
is the constant surface density of the infinite uniform plate. The translation symmetry implies that the potential due to the uniform infinite plane must be a function of the height alone. However since
\nabla^{2}\Phi(z)=\Phi^{\prime\prime}(z)
, the only harmonic function
\Phi(z)
of the height alone is the linear function of
z
. If
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
, equation (
VIII.9
) is technically an infinite constant, which is invalid. However the
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
case is the scale-free Mestel disk, and its potential is already provided in equation (
II.7
), which follows equation (
VIII.7
) via the subtraction of the infinite offset (see eq.
II.5
).

VIII.2
Algebraic cases with the oblate spheroidal coordinates

The same change of the variable,
t=x(x+\sigma)/(x+x_{0})
with
2x_{0}=\sigma+|\zeta|
transforms equation (
VIII.4
) to

\frac{-\Phi(\bm{r})}{G\Lambda_{0}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}x\frac{[x(x+\sigma)]^{2\beta-1}(x+x_{0})^{2\gamma-2}}{[x^{2}(x+\sigma)^{2}+(x+x_{0})^{2}]^{\beta+\gamma-1}}.

(VIII.10)

If both
2\beta
and
\beta+\gamma
are integers, the integrand is a rational function of
x
and the integral is resolved into an elementary algebraic function involving up to logarithms or inverse trigonometric/hyperbolic functions. The reduction is achieved via the integration by parts and the partial fraction decomposition utilizing the factorization of the quartic as in

x^{2}(x+\sigma)^{2}+(x+x_{0})^{2}\\
=[(x+x_{0}-\xi^{1/2}n_{+})^{2}+n_{+}^{2}][(x+x_{0}+\xi^{1/2}n_{-})^{2}+n_{-}^{2}].

Here
2n_{\pm}=1\pm\chi^{1/2}
, and
(\xi,\chi)=(\alpha-1,1-\delta)
is the
oblate
spheroidal coordinate component
5
5

5

This definition, consistent with the notation of
Evans & de Zeeuw 1992
, differs from
(
An 2026
, sect. III.A)
in the square roots for
\alpha
and
\delta
as well as the overall scaling by
R_{0}
. This choice makes clear the manifest correspondence to the prolate spheroidal coordinate defined in Sect.
V.2
.
defined such that

(\alpha^{1/2},\delta^{1/2})=\left(\frac{\upsilon_{+}+\upsilon_{-}}{2},\frac{\upsilon_{+}-\upsilon_{-}}{2}\right),

(VIII.11)

where
\upsilon_{\pm}^{2}=(\varrho\pm 1)^{2}+\zeta^{2}
.
Also note that
(\alpha\delta,\xi\chi)=(\varrho^{2},\zeta^{2})
and
\sigma^{2}=\varrho^{2}+\zeta^{2}=\alpha+\delta-1=\xi-\chi+1
, and
